4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are
completed by the Applicant, which do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Application Type 505(b)(1) New Drug Application (New Molecular Entity)
Application Number(s) 219304
Priority or Standard Priority
Submit Date(s) June 17, 2024
Received Date(s) June 17, 2024
PDUFA Goal Date February 17, 2025
Division/Office Division of Oncology 3/Office of Oncology Drugs
Review Completion Date See stamp date
Established Name Vimseltinib
(Proposed) Trade Name ROMVIMZA
Pharmacologic Class kinase inhibitor
Code name DCC-3014
Applicant Deciphera Pharmaceuticals
Formulation(s) Capsules: 30 mg, 20 mg, 14 mg
Dosing Regimen 30 mg orally twice weekly
Applicant Proposed Is a kinase inhibitor indicated for treatment of adult patients
Indication(s)/Population(s) with symptomatic tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT).
Recommendation on Traditional Approval
Regulatory Action
Recommended Is a kinase inhibitor indicated for treatment of adult patients
Indication(s)/Population(s) with symptomatic tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT) for
(if applicable) which surgical resection will potentially cause worsening
functional limitation or severe morbidity

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Vimseltinib is a new molecular entity (NME), pursuant to regulations under 21 CFR 601.
Vimseltinib (ROMVIMZA®) is an oral, small molecule kinase inhibitor of colony-stimulating
factor 1 receptor (CSF1R). At the time of the New Drug Application (NDA) submission on June
17, 2024, vimseltinib was not approved for any indication in the United States or elsewhere.
The Applicant’s proposed indication at the time of the NDA submission was:
ROMVIMZA is a kinase inhibitor indicated for treatment of adult patients with symptomatic
tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT).

1.2

Conclusion on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness

The review team concluded that the submitted data provide substantial evidence of efficacy with
acceptable safety of vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for
which surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe
morbidity. This conclusion is based on the results of Part 1 of Study DCC-3014-03-001
(MOTION), a double-blind, multicenter, randomized (2:1), placebo-controlled study in 123 adult
patients with symptomatic TGCT for which surgical resection would potentially cause worsening
functional limitation or severe morbidity, as judged by surgical consultation or a
multidisciplinary tumor board. Symptomatic disease was defined as experiencing at least
moderate pain or moderate stiffness within the screening period. Pain was assessed using the
Brief Pain Inventory (BPI) Worst Pain numeric rating scale (NRS); stiffness was assessed using
the Worst Stiffness NRS. A score of 4 or more out of 10 (with 10 being the worst condition) on
either scale was required to define moderate pain or stiffness for inclusion purposes. Patients
received vimseltinib (n=83) 30 mg twice weekly or placebo (n=40) through Week 24 during the
double-blind (Part 1) part of the study. During the open-label period (Part 2), patients could
continue to receive vimseltinib or crossover from placebo to receive vimseltinib until disease
progression or unacceptable toxicity. The primary efficacy endpoint was objective response rate
(ORR) at Week 25 as assessed by a blinded independent radiological review (IRC) according to
Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) v1.1.
The ORR in the primary efficacy analysis population of MOTION for the vimseltinib arm was
40% (95% confidence interval [CI]: 29, 51) with 4 (5%) patients experiencing a complete
response. Of the 33 patients who experienced an objective response who were followed for an
additional 6 months from the time of ORR primary analysis, duration of response (DOR) was 6
months for 85% of patients and 9 months for 58% of patients. There were no responders in the
placebo arm.

Sections 8.1.1. and 8.1.2
TGCT is a rare, serious condition that is associated with significant morbidity; patients with
TGCT represent a population with a high unmet medical need. The Applicant has demonstrated
substantial evidence of effectiveness of vimseltinib in patients with TGCT based on clinically
meaningful durable tumor shrinkage accompanied by supportive improvements in clinical
outcome assessments including ROM, physical function, and pain. Therefore, the review team
recommends granting traditional approval to vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with
symptomatic TGCT for which surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional
limitation or severe morbidity. The review team modified the indication statement from the
Applicant’s proposed indication of “treatment of adult patients with symptomatic tenosynovial
giant cell tumor (TGCT)”. In addition to requiring patients to have symptomatic disease as
described previously, an additional inclusion criterion for patients enrolling in MOTION was
that surgical resection would potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe
morbidity. The review team regarded this additional criterion to be essential as part of the
indicated patient population given that local treatment with surgery can be effective if feasible,
and patients for whom surgery was likely to result in good functional outcomes were not studied
in MOTION. As such the review team revised the indication statement accordingly.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Tenosynovial giant cell tumors (TGCTs) are a rare, locally aggressive, non-malignant tumor that typically arises from the joints, bursa and
tendon sheaths. TGCT is not life-threatening and does not metastasize but may cause significant morbidity impacting a patient’s quality of life
(Stacchiotti 2023). It exhibits a female predilection (2:1) and primarily affects patients between 20 and 50 years, although it can occur at any age.
Because of the rarity of TGCT, there is very limited data in the literature regarding predilection for race or ethnicity. One study conducted in 83
patients with TGCT who received pexidartinib showed that 65% of patients were White, 18% per Other. 8% were Black, and 1.2% were missing.
There were no Native Americans or Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander reported (Lin 2024). There are two subtypes of TGCT, diffuse and
localized. Diffuse TGCT (dTGCT) tends to affect the larger joints, most commonly the knee and tends to be locally aggressive and have a higher
recurrence rate of approximately 55%. Localized (lTGCT) disease commonly affects the digits and the wrists and has a recurrence rate of 15%
(Ehrenstein 2017). The estimated overall annual incidence in the United States (US) of 11 cases per million; 1.8 cases per million for diffuse-type
TGCT and 9.2 cases per million for localized TGCT (Lopez-Bastida 2021). From the largest TCGT registry worldwide, the Danish study
reported the prevalence of lTGCT and dTGCT as 44/100,000 and 11/100,000, respectively. The 5-year recurrence-free survival (RFS) for lTGCT
is 70–90 % and 30–80 % for dTGCT (Stacchiotti 2023).
Surgery remains the mainstay of treatment for TGCT; however, there is no consensus on the optimal treatment as surgery can lead to joint
damage, functional limitations, and recurrence (Stacchiotti 2023). In TGCT, a translocation in CSF1-COL6A3, t(1;2)(p13;q35-37), leads to
overexpression of colony stimulating factor 1 (CSF1). In patients with unresectable relapsed and/or recurrent TGCT, agents that inhibit CSF1R,
represent a logical therapeutic approach (Tap 2015). Pexidartinib was approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for patients with
TGCT associated with severe morbidity or functional limitations and not amenable to surgery. However, in the pivotal study to support approval,
ENLIVEN, 3 of 61 (5%) patients who received pexidartinib developed signs of serious liver injury. Hepatotoxicity including liver failure and
life-threatening vanishing bile duct syndrome, ductopenia, and symptomatic cholestasis can occur. Thus, pexidartinib has a boxed warning for
hepatotoxicity and a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) with Elements to Assure Safe Use (ETASU) to mitigate the risk of serious
and potentially fatal liver injury. Per the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines for Soft Tissue Sarcoma v1.2023, other
systemic treatment options include nilotinib and imatinib in certain circumstances. Radiation therapy may be in used in some certain
circumstances including patients with relapsed disease or when surgery results in residual tumor. It has been reported to potentially reduce the
risk of recurrence (Griffin 2012 and Stacchiotti 2023).

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

The data submitted by the Applicant to support the efficacy of vimseltinib in patients with TGCT come from the MOTION study, an
international, multicenter, randomized, doubleဨblind, placeboဨcontrolled study in 123 adult patients with symptomatic TGCT and forms the
primary basis on which the Applicant is seeking approval of vimseltinib in the proposed indication. The major efficacy endpoint was ORR
assessed by blinded IRC via RECIST v1.1. ORR at Week 25 in patients who received vimseltinib compared to placebo was 40% (95% CI: 29,
51) with 4 (5%) experiencing a complete response for patients on the vimseltinib arm and 0% (95% CI: 0, 9) for patients on the placebo arm (pvalue <0.0001). Of the 33 patients who experienced an objective response who were followed for an additional 6 months from the time of ORR
primary analysis, duration of response was 6 months for 85% of patients and 9 months for 58% of patients. To support an overall favorable
risk:benefit based on the effect on ORR, FDA conducted analyses on active ROM and PROs using the PROMIS-PF and BPI-30. The primary
endpoint of ORR was supported by statistically significant improvements in active range of motion, patient-reported physical functioning, and
patient-reported pain observed in the vimseltinib arm compared to the placebo arm at Week 25.
Safety data supporting the proposed indication reflected exposure to vimseltinib in patients enrolled on the MOTION study (83 patients exposed
to vimseltinib and 38 patients exposed to placebo). The most common adverse events for patients treated with vimseltinib included increased
aspartate aminotransferase (AST), periorbital edema, fatigue, rash, increased cholesterol, peripheral edema, face edema, decreased neutrophils,
decreased leukocytes, pruritus, and increased alanine aminotransferase (ALT).
An additional 135 patients with TGCT or other solid tumors from study DCC-3014-01-001, an open-label, single arm, dose escalation and
expansion study evaluating vimseltinib in patients with advanced solid tumors including TGCT were evaluated in the integrated summary of
safety.
Both pexidartinib and vimseltinib are kinase inhibitors that inhibit CSF1R. Given the important identified risk of severe hepatotoxicity observed
with the use of pexidartinib, FDA performed a comprehensive analysis of the risk of hepatotoxicity associated with vimseltinib. FDA considered
the need for a Boxed Warning and REMS based on a possible class effect and the identified risk for hepatotoxicity with use of pexidartinib.
However, the safety data from the 253 patients who received at least one dose of vimseltinib in MOTION (including 35 patients who crossed
over to receive vimseltinib during the open-label portion) or in the supportive study DCC-3014-01-001 did not identify a risk of serious or
significant hepatotoxicity that would require a REMS or a boxed warning. While Grade 1 AST or ALT elevations were common, these did not
lead to clinically significant hepatic toxicity or liver injury, with no cases of Hy’s law observed. The Applicant argued that the mechanism of
action for drug induced liver injury observed with pexidartinib (specifically, cholestatic toxicity) may be due to its unique biomolecular
21

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
characteristics, including metabolites, rather than due to a class effect of agents targeting the CSF1R pathway. While the FDA reviewed the
literature and concluded that the presence of unique metabolites might possibly explain pexidartinib-associated hepatotoxicity, the review team
concluded that the nonclinical and clinical data could not fully rule out the risk for hepatotoxicity with this class of drugs including vimseltinib.
Therefore, the review team included a section 5 warning regarding hepatotoxicity in product labeling (See Sections 5 and 8.2.5 for details).
Additionally, a post-marketing requirement (PMR) registry study collecting data on liver function for an additional at least 100 patients dosed
with vimseltinib in the postmarketing setting was agreed upon for this application. FDA also instituted an enhanced pharmacovigilance plan to
capture hepatotoxicity in the postmarketing setting for patients not enrolled on the registry (See Sections 8.2.5 and 13 for details).
Additional warnings and precautions included in section 5 of product labeling are embryo-fetal toxicity, allergic reactions to FD&C Yellow No.5
and No.6 and increased creatinine without impact on renal function.
A favorable risk:benefit assessment has been established for vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for which
surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional limitation based on the results of a single study, MOTION. Given the rarity of
TGCT and small sample size, there are limited data to draw conclusions regarding subgroup analyses (i.e., race, age, sex, etc.) from this
application.
Postmarketing requirements for vimseltinib include the PMR registry study for hepatotoxicity described above, a PMR evaluating vimseltinib in
participants with moderate hepatic impairment, a carcinogenicity study, and 3 PMRs to evaluate the potential for drug-drug interactions (with
substrates of BCRP, OATP1B1/OATP1B3, and OCT2).

Conclusions and Reasons

Patients experience pain, stiffness, swelling and reduced ROM that
impacts quality of life.
x The annual incidence of TGCT in the United States is estimated to be
11 cases per million people. This is based on an estimate that 9.2
cases per million are localized TGCTs and 1.8 cases per million are
diffuse-type TGCTs.
x Recurrence rates are variable and have been reported to be 15% for
localized disease and 55% for diffuse disease. Even higher rates of
recurrence have been reported.

Current
Treatment
Options

x Excision of tumor is the mainstay of treatment. Patients may require
repeated surgical procedures, sometimes leading to loss of function,
joint replacement, or amputation.
x Pexidartinib is the only FDA-approved systemic therapy in this setting,
demonstrating an ORR of 38% (95% CI: 27, 50), with 22/23
responders with >6 months of follow-up maintaining response for 6
months. Pexidartinib is available to patients only through
implementation of a REMS due to severe and life-threatening liver
toxicity, with an associated boxed warning.
x Imatinib and nilotinib are listed in the NCCN guidelines for soft tissue
sarcoma as useful in certain circumstances.
x Radiation therapy may be reserved for those who are not surgical
candidates and may reduce the risk of recurrence.

There is an unmet medical need for new
effective systemic treatments for patients
with TGCT. Due to the nature of TGCT
tumors, complete surgical resection is
often challenging and the risk of
recurrence is variable and has been
reported to be as high as 70%. The only
approved drug for this condition is not curative
and can cause life-threatening hepatotoxicity.

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT) is a rare tumor arising from the synovium of joints,
bursae, and tendon sheaths (de St. Aubain Somerhausen and van de Rijn, 2013) caused by
dysregulation of the colony stimulating factor 1 (CSF1) gene which results in overproduction of
CSF1 and recruitment of CSF1 receptor (CSF1R)-dependent inflammatory cells into the affected
joint (van der Heijden et al, 2023; West et al, 2006). The lesion can either present as a single
nodule (localized form) or as multiple nodules (diffuse form) along a synovial layer or tendon
sheath. The annual incidence rate of TGCT in the United States (US) is reported as
approximately 11 per million (localized: 9.2 per million and diffuse TGCT: 1.8 per million)
(Myers and Masi, 1980), with a more recent epidemiological study from the Netherlands
reporting a higher worldwide incidence of 43 per million (digits: 29 per million; localized
extremities: 10 per million; and diffuse TGCT: 4 per million) (Mastboom et al, 2017). Surgical
resection is considered the first treatment option; however, patients may present with inoperable
disease or may not be suitable candidates for surgery. Surgery for such patients could cause
significant functional limitation or severe morbidity. In addition, relapse rates have been reported
for up to 50% of diffuse-type TGCT patients, requiring repetitive surgical treatment that can lead
to substantial damage to the affected joint and impaired quality of life (QoL) (Gouin and
Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016). As patients are typically diagnosed early in life (35 to
50 years old), limitations from increased morbidity can have a significant impact on QoL
(Mastboom et al, 2018, Stacchiotti et al, 2023).
Although TGCT is not life threatening, patients carry the burden of requiring antiဩinflammatory
and analgesic medications, including opioids, to alleviate the severe pain and debilitation.
Additionally, for patients who undergo multiple surgeries, there is also the burden of
rehabilitation and recovery after every procedure, which also contributes to associated
morbidities. Thus, systemic disease targeted therapy is necessary for patients with inoperable or
recurrent TGCT.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with applicant’s overall assessment. Recurrence rate for patients with localized
TGCT is lower than patients with diffuse TGCT (dTGCT), and the reported rate of recurrence
for patients with dTGCT has been reported to be as high as 70% (Stacchiotti 2023). In this rare
tumor, little data is reported on the race and ethnicity of patients with TGCT. One study
conducted in 83 patients with TGCT who received pexidartinib, showed that 65% of patients
were White, 18% per Other. 8% were Black, and 1.2% were missing. There were no Native
28
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
Americans or Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander reported. Most patients (69% of
vimseltinib treated, 70% of placebo treated) had diffuse TGCT. In all patients, the most
commonly affected joint was knee (68% in both vimseltinib and placebo treated), followed by
ankle (11%, 15%) and hip (13%, 2.5%). Mean age at time of enrollment is similar in patients
treated with vimseltinib (44, range: 20-78 years old) and placebo (42.5, range: 21-72)

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
Surgery remains the recommended treatment for all forms of TGCT, when feasible. Localized
TGCT can generally be surgically removed, but removal of diffuse TGCT may be challenging
because the diffuse nature of the lesions may make full excision difficult (van der Heijden et al,
2023). Retrospective analysis of patients with newly diagnosed or first relapse TGCT was
conducted to understand surgical outcomes and shows most patients (85%) with localized form
of TGCT have a complete resection, while only 37% of patients with diffuse type of TGCT have
a complete resection (Palmerini et al, 2015).
Recurrence is observed in both forms of the disease following surgery. In localized TGCT,
reported recurrence rate is approximately 10% after surgery (Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et
al, 2016; van der Heijden et al, 2023). Recurrence of localized TGCT is strongly associated with
a negative outcome. Approximately 43% of patients who experience a relapse, even after a
complete surgical resection, have a subsequent relapse within 5 years (Palmerini et al, 2015).
Recurrence rates are considerably higher for diffuse TGCT, up to 55% (Ehrenstein et al, 2017;
Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016) and a relapse-free survival rate of approximately
40% in 10 years (van der Heijden et al, 2023). Patients with recurring TGCT may require
repetitive surgical treatment that can lead to substantial damage to the affected joint and impaired
QoL (Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016; Mastboom et al, 2018). Eventually, some
patients may require limb amputation for severe complications or present with metastatic disease
(Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016). Multiple surgeries owing to recurrence may not
be possible for patients who have significant risks associated with open surgery (eg, older
patients, patients with comorbidities), or present with inoperable disease. Surgery for such
patients could cause significant functional limitation or severe morbidity.
Systemic therapies have been tested in patients with locally advanced or relapsed diffuse TGCT
(Brahmi et al, 2016), including kinase inhibitors that non-selectively target the CSF1/CSF1R axis
(eg, imatinib and nilotinib), and monoclonal antibodies targeting CSF1R expression (eg,
emactuzumab, and cabiralizumab) (Stacchiotti et al, 2023; Gronchi et al, 2021). These kinase
inhibitors have not been developed for the treatment of TGCT and have been accessed outside of
approved labeling for the treatment of TGCT, and monoclonal antibodies are investigational.
Best clinical practices have justified the use of systemic therapies in symptomatic TGCT based
on the location and extent of the disease, particularly if disease progression or surgical
intervention may result in joint damage and affect QoL (Stacchiotti et al, 2023).
Pexidartinib (TURALIO®), a kinase inhibitor of CSF1R, cellular homolog of the feline sarcoma
viral oncogene v-kit (KIT), and FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 (FLT3), was approved by the Food
29
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
and Drug Administration (FDA) in 2019 for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic
TGCT associated with severe morbidity or functional limitations and not amenable to
improvement with surgery. The approval was based on the results of the placebo-controlled,
double-blind Phase 3 study (ENLIVEN) showing an objective response rate (ORR) of 38% for
the pexidartinib arm over 0% in the placebo arm at study Week 25. However, pexidartinib can
cause serious and potentially fatal liver injury and its use requires frequent liver tests for
monitoring of liver toxicity. Pexidartinib’s prescribing information includes a black box warning
of hepatotoxicity and is only available to patients in the United States through a risk evaluation
and mitigation strategy (REMS) program (TURALIO USPI, 2023).
A high unmet medical need remains for a selective CSF1R inhibitor with a more favorable
risk/benefit profile.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of currently available treatments. In many patients,
surgery is potentially curative, and surgery is often recommended as first-line treatment in
patients for whom surgery would not worsen morbidity of functioning. Surgical options may
include marginal excision with synovectomy, debulking surgery, total joint replacement and in
rare cases, amputation. In patients for whom surgery is not an option, pexidartinib is the only
FDA-approved drug; however, pexidartinib is only available through a REMS program and has a
box warning due to risk of serious, potentially irreversible, liver injury, including vanishing bile
duct syndrome. Pexidartinib was approved based on a multi-center, double-blind, placebocontrolled study which demonstrated an ORR per RECIST at Week 25 of 38% (95% CI: 27, 50).
Twenty-two of the 23 patients who achieved a response and had been followed for at least six
months had maintained the response for 6 months. Additionally, patients who received
pexidartinib experienced a statistically significant improvement in range of motion.
Per NCCN guidelines, imatinib and nilotinib are other systemic therapies that are useful in
certain circumstances. In certain circumstances, radiation can be considered for patients with
diffuse or recurrent TGCT, though data does not currently support inclusion of radiotherapy as a
standard treatment. Postsurgical external beam radiation has been shown to improve functional
status and provide local control in some patients (Baniel 2023). In all patients with TGCT, active
surveillance may be considered, and the risks and benefits of potential treatment must be
evaluated for each individual patient. Patients with a low symptom burden and patients for whom
surgery would likely not be curative and may increase morbidity may benefit from active
surveillance.

The FDA’s Assessment:
1. In general, FDA agrees with Applicant’s assessment with the following additions noted
by the reviewer: Type C Meeting, March 3, 2020: FDA recommended the Applicant
consider a study that includes an internal control and stated that FDA was open to a
randomized controlled study with a provision for patients crossing over following a
limited treatment course for patients randomized to placebo. FDA stated that patients
must have surgical consultation prior to enrollment in MOTION, as surgery is potentially
curative in some patients.
2. Type C Meeting, July 22, 2020: FDA provided extensive feedback on COA and PRO
elements. FDA agreed that proposed concepts of interest (ROM, physical function, pain,
stiffness, swelling and joint instability) are important and relevant to the experience of
patients with TGCT. FDA requested additional information on the proposed inclusion of
qualitative data via the 15 PROMIS-PF measure, which Deciphera provided. FDA agreed
with the use of PROMIS-PF and noted that the assessment may bias towards showing
less of a response in patients with upper extremity TGCT. FDA agreed that the worst pain
NRS was a content-valid item. FDA recommended that interpretation of worst stiffness
and worst swelling be done with consideration for potential confounders and noted that
qualitative data in RA suggests that patients may not be able to differentiate between
stiffness, pain, and swelling. The Applicant proposed removal of the question regarding
joint instability, and FDA agreed.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Vimseltinib is a small molecule kinase inhibitor of colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor
(CSF1R). The established pharmacologic class is kinase inhibitor.
Figure 1

Chemical Structure of Vimseltinib

(Excerpted from Applicant’s Submission)

Target Background
Colony stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R), also known as CD115 (Cluster of Differentiation
115) or macrophage colony-stimulating factor receptor (M-CSFR), is a tyrosine kinase
transmembrane receptor encoded by the human CSF1R gene (also known as c-FMS). CSF1R
signaling is involved in macrophage and osteoclast development and differentiation. In
tenosynovial giant cell tumors, colony stimulating factor 1 (CSF-1) is overexpressed.
Overexpression of CSF1 and excess signaling through CSF1R may enhance tumor-associated
macrophage (TAM)-mediated tumor growth, angiogenesis, and immunosuppression.
Pharmacology
Vimseltinib inhibited CSF1R with an IC50 of 2.8 nM. In vitro, vimseltinib inhibited
autophosphorylation and phosphorylation of CSF1R in THP-1 cells (a cell line used to study
monocyte and macrophage function) and ERK signaling in whole blood monocytes. Vimseltinib
had anti-tumor activity, inhibiting CSF1R-dependent proliferation in vitro in M-NFS-60
myelogenous leukemia cells. In an in vivo mouse model, administration of vimseltinib reduced
levels of CSF1R-dependent cFOS transcription.
ADME/PK
A single IV dose of 1 mg/kg vimseltinib had 73% CNS permeability in Sprague Dawley rats.
General toxicology
Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in rats (4-weeks, 13-weeks, and 26-weeks) and
dogs (4-weeks, 13-weeks, and 39-weeks). The indication did not warrant repeat-dose toxicology
studies of more than 3 months duration.
Rats exhibited similar findings across studies:

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity

Rats were more sensitive to the effects of vimseltinib compared to dogs.
Dogs were dosed higher in the 4-week study (up to 25 mg/kg/day) vs. the 13-week and 39-week
studies (up to 8 mg/kg/day) and exhibited more severe toxicities at higher doses. Considering all
three studies, findings included:
x

decreases in phosphate, calcium, and APTT
increases in AST, ALT, GLDH, creatine kinase, fibrinogen, reticulocytes, and platelets
edema in the skin, skin de-pigmentation (females only), excessive lacrimation, and
epididymal mineralization

The vimseltinib toxicity profile is characterized by inflammation, hematologic, hepatic, renal,
bone, and gastrointestinal toxicities, and is generally consistent with CSF1R inhibition. Findings
in animals indicate vimseltinib has the potential to cause hepatotoxicity, which may be secondary
to inflammation. Another small molecule kinase inhibitor of CSF1R is pexidartinib. Literature
(Qin et al., 2023) suggests that the mechanism of action for pexidartinib-mediated liver injury
may be due to its unique biomolecular characteristics, including metabolites, rather than due to a
class effect alone. While plausible, the totality of the nonclinical data for vimseltinib do not fully
rule out the risk for hepatotoxicity in humans.
General toxicology findings reflected in USPI sections 8.4 and 13.2
In a 26-week repeat-dose general toxicology study in rats, chronic progressive nephropathy
occurred at vimseltinib doses of 2.5 mg/kg/day (approximately 6 times the exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC). Degeneration of blood vessels (perivascular inflammation
and necrosis of arteries and arteriole walls) occurred in multiple tissues at 5 mg/kg/day
vimseltinib (approximately 12 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
Rats administered vimseltinib at 2.5 mg/kg had physeal thickening and decay of the incisors
and molars. Bone and tooth toxicities occurred at exposures approximately 8 times the
37
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
recommended dose based on AUC. These findings are relevant to pediatric use.
In a 39-week repeat-dose general toxicology study in dogs, skin depigmentation in the head and
legs occurred at vimseltinib doses of 4 mg/kg/day corresponding to exposures lower than the
exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC.
Genotoxicity
Vimseltinib was not mutagenic in the bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames). In an in vitro
micronucleus assay, vimseltinib increased micronuclei after a 24-hour incubation in the absence
of metabolic activation. In vivo, vimseltinib administered to rats at doses up to 200 mg/kg/day
did not increase bone marrow micronucleated polychromatic erythrocytes, nor did vimseltinib
increase liver DNA strand breaks. The overall assessment was that vimseltinib was negative for
genotoxicity.
Carcinogenicity
In a 6-month transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study at doses up to 12.5 mg/kg/day, vimseltinib
was negative for carcinogenic findings. The Applicant is currently conducting a 104-week
carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley rats to be submitted as a PMR.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
In a rat embryo-fetal development study, pregnant female rats were dosed once daily during the
period of organogenesis (gestational days 6 to 17) at doses of 2.5, 5, or 15 mg/kg/day. Structural
abnormalities (skeletal variations) occurred at 2.5 mg/kg/day (approximately 3 times the
exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). Additional structural abnormalities (cardiac
malformations) were observed at the highest dose of 15 mg/kg/day (approximately 23 times the
exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
Based on data from animal studies and its mechanism of action, vimseltinib can cause fetal harm.
Females of reproductive potential and males with female partners of reproductive potential are
recommended to use effective contraception during treatment with vimseltinib and for 1 month
after the last dose. The duration of contraception is based on 5 times the clinical elimination halflife of 6 days.
There are no data on the presence of vimseltinib or its metabolites in either human or animal
milk or its effects on a breastfed child or on milk production. Because of the potential for serious
adverse reactions in the breastfed child, women are recommended not to breastfeed during
treatment with vimseltinib and for 1 month after the last dose.
Based on findings from animal studies, vimseltinib may impair fertility.
In a fertility and early embryonic development study, male rats were administered 1, 2.5, or 5
mg/kg/day of vimseltinib starting 10 weeks before cohabitation, during cohabitation with

untreated females, and at least 2 weeks post-cohabitation. Lower epididymal and testes weights
were observed at 5 mg/kg/day (approximately 12 times the exposure at the recommended dose
based on AUC). There were no treatment-related effects on mating, fertility, or sperm parameters
at any dose tested. Female rats were administered 2.5, 5, or 10 mg/kg/day of vimseltinib 2 weeks
prior to cohabitation with untreated males and during cohabitation until gestational day 7.
Although there were no treatment-related effects on mating or estrous cycles, vimseltinib
administered daily resulted in post-implantation loss at 10 mg/kg/day in female rats
(approximately 20 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
In a 26-week repeat-dose general toxicology study, recovery male rats that were administered 2.5
or 5 mg/kg/day had moderate to marked reductions in sperm and marked testicular atrophy (1 of
5 and 2 of 5 animals, respectively) corresponding to approximately 6 and 12 times the exposure
at the recommended dose based on AUC, respectively. In a 39-week repeat-dose general
toxicology study, minimal to moderate epididymal mineralization occurred in male dogs
administered 4 mg/kg/day corresponding to exposures lower than the exposure at the
recommended dose based on AUC.

Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Primary pharmacology
40
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
Vimseltinib selectively and potently inhibits CSF1R in recombinant kinase assays and in cellular
assays with CSF1R-expressing cells, including monocytic cell lines, osteoclast precursors, and
monocytes from freshly drawn whole blood samples. In vivo, pharmacokinetic/
pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) analysis of vimseltinib inhibition of CSF1R signaling demonstrates
potent and durable inhibition of CSF1R. Vimseltinib depletes tumor-associated macrophages
(TAMs) in an immunocompetent colorectal cancer model. In a prostate cancer model, wherein
primary tumor growth results in bone invasion and osteoclast-mediated bone degradation,
vimseltinib treatment demonstrates statistically significant protection of bone degradation. DP7005, a metabolite of vimseltinib, is an inhibitor of CSF1R kinase in enzyme and cell-based
assays.
In Vitro Pharmacology
Vimseltinib is a potent inhibitor of CSF1R kinase in vitro in both recombinant enzyme and cell
based assays. Vimseltinib inhibits CSF1-stimulated CSF1R autophosphorylation in a monocytic
cell line, inhibits cellular proliferation of a monocytic cell line dependent on CSF1R signaling,
inhibits CSF1-stimulated osteoclast differentiation, and decreases macrophage-mediated tumor
cell migration. Vimseltinib also inhibits CSF1R signaling in monocytes in an in vitro human
whole blood assay.

osteoclasts. Vimseltinib inhibited osteoclast differentiation with IC50 = 9.3 nM. Study DCC 3014-02-0008, eCTD 4.2.1.1.
Vimseltinib inhibited macrophage-dependent tumor cell migration by >50% at 100 nM, the only concentration tested. Study
DCC-3014-02-0022, eCTD 4.2.1.1.
Vimseltinib inhibited CSF1R signaling in monocytes in human whole blood with an average IC50 = 310 ± 84 nM. Study
DCC 3014-02-0007, eCTD 4.2.1.1.
Vimseltinib significantly inhibited CSF1 stimulated expression of cFOS mRNA for >24 h post dose at all doses (30, 15, 7.5,
and 3.75 mg/kg single dose PO). Study DCC-3014-02-0012, eCTD 4.2.1.1. In a multiple dosing study, after dosing at 10
mg/kg orally for 6 days, vimseltinib inhibited CSF1-stimulated cFOS expression by >80% for >48 h after the last dose.
After dosing at 3 mg/kg, vimseltinib inhibited cFOS expression by 90% or greater for 12 h, and ~50% at 24 h and 48 h.
After dosing at 1 mg/kg, vimseltinib inhibited cFOS expression by ~80% for 6 h after dosing but did not significantly inhibit
at time points >6 h. Study DCC-3014-02-0013, eCTD 4.2.1.1.
In the PC3 prostate cancer model (where tumor growth leads to local invasion and bone degradation), treatment with
vimseltinib at 30 mg/kg, BID produced statistically significant (p<0.0001) prevention of bone degradation. Study DCC3014-02-0014, eCTD 4.2.1.1. In a second study, treatment with vimseltinib at 10 mg/kg, administered once or twice daily
also led to statistically significant prevention of bone degradation (p<0.0001). Study DCC-3014-02-0033, eCTD 4.2.1.1.

j

(b) (4)

at 10 mg/kg PO, QD. Study DCC-301402-0015, eCTD 4.2.1.1.

In Vivo Pharmacology
Vimseltinib was well tolerated in all mouse pharmacology studies. In a PK/PD model,
vimseltinib significantly inhibited CSF1-stimulated expression of cellular homolog of the Finkel
Biskis-Jinkins murine osteogenic sarcoma virus oncogene (cFOS) mRNA for >24 h postdose at
all doses, as low as 3.75 mg/kg PO, after a single dose. After dosing at 10 mg/kg orally for 6
days, vimseltinib inhibited CSF1-stimulated cFOS expression by >80% for >48 h after the last
dose (Figure 3 and Table 4). After dosing at 3 mg/kg, vimseltinib inhibited cFOS expression by
90% or greater for 12 h, and approximately 50% at 24 h and 48 h. The 24 h area under the
concentration time/curve (AUC) for the 3 mg/kg group was 49,314 ng*h/mL (Table 5). The half
maximally effective concentration (EC50) was 430 ng/mL and the eighty percent maximally
effective concentration (EC80) was 1,700 ng/mL.

Secondary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
In a large panel of kinase activity assays, vimseltinib exhibits >100-fold selectivity for CSF1R
kinase versus 298 other human kinases, including its closely related family members KIT,
platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha (PDGFRĮ), PDGFR beta (PDGFRȕ), and FLT3
(DCC-3014-02-0002, DCC-3014-02-0003, and DCC-3014-02-0031, eCTD 4.2.1.2; Figure 7).
Vimseltinib was evaluated versus a battery of 135 receptors, ion channels, and enzymes (DCC3014-02-0010, eCTD 4.2.1.2). Among the battery of 104 receptor binding assays profiled,
vimseltinib inhibited MT3 (ML2) (agonist radioligand) by 68.2% when evaluated at 10 μM
concentration. All other receptor binding assays were inhibited <25% by vimseltinib. Among the
battery of 31 enzyme and uptake assays, vimseltinib exhibited 86.2% inhibition of ABL kinase,
42.8% inhibition of FYN kinase, and 90.6% inhibition of LYN kinase when evaluated at 10 μM
concentration. These kinases were further tested, wherein IC50 values of 2,200 nM, >3,300 nM,

Safety Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
Vimseltinib was assessed in nonclinical safety pharmacology studies including evaluation of
neurobehavioral effects and evaluation of impact on cardiovascular parameters and respiratory
function. There were no effects of vimseltinib on central nervous system (CNS) function in rats
at single doses up to 30 mg/kg, the highest dose tested. A mildly higher tidal volume was noted
in rats given 30 mg/kg in the respiratory safety pharmacology study, without an effect on
respiratory rate or minute volume. The 30 mg/kg/day dose exceeds the severely toxic dose in
10% of the evaluation cohort (STD10) determined in the definitive Good Laboratory Practice
(GLP) compliant 28-day rat study. In the dog cardiovascular (CV) safety pharmacology study,
no vimseltinib-related qualitative electrocardiogram (ECG) effects or quantitative changes in PR
interval, QRS duration, QT interval, QT interval corrected by Fridericia’s formula (QTcF)
interval, heart rate (HR), arterial pressure, or body temperature were seen at doses up to 15
mg/kg. In the in vitro human ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) study, the half maximal
inhibitory concentration (IC50) values for inhibition of the hERG potassium current by
vimseltinib and DP-7005 could not be calculated due to 17% or less inhibition at the maximum
concentrations that could be tested at the solubility limits of the two compounds (30 ȝM). Based

on these results, vimseltinib appears unlikely to have significant CNS, respiratory, or

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
The absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) of vimseltinib and its active
metabolite, DP-7005, were characterized in a range of in vitro and in vivo studies to support the
proposed clinical indication and dosing regimen. The absorption of vimseltinib has been
characterized in an extensive program of single-dose PK studies and repeated-dose toxicokinetic
(TK) studies in mouse, rat, dog, and monkey. The distribution of vimseltinib was evaluated in a
quantitative whole-body autoradiography study and vimseltinib’s ability to penetrate the CNS
was assessed in rats. Plasma protein binding was evaluated in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and
humans. The characterization of vimseltinib’s metabolites were assessed in vitro with mouse, rat,
dog, monkey, and human hepatocytes and in vivo in dogs and bile duct-cannulated (BDC) rats.
Metabolic stability was evaluated in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human liver microsomes and
hepatocytes while cytochrome P450 enzyme (CYP) reaction phenotyping was assessed in human
liver microsomes and recombinant human CYP enzymes. CYP induction and inhibition were
evaluated using human hepatocytes and liver microsomes, respectively. The excretion of
vimseltinib was evaluated in dogs and in bile-duct cannulated rats. The potential for vimseltinib
to affect transporter activity was evaluated in vitro.

Absorption
The PK parameters of vimseltinib following oral and IV administration of vimseltinib in male Sprague Dawley rats (DCC-3014-03-0012) and male beagle dogs (DCC3014-03-0014) are provided in Table 7. PK parameters in rats following oral administration were similar to IV, with the exception that Tmax occurred at 2.67-24 hours,
depending on dose level, instead of immediately after infusion. The PK of DP-7005 was evaluated in dog studies in addition to vimseltinib after dog microsomal studies
indicated substantial formation of the metabolite DP-7005.
A key finding from the single dose oral PK studies in rats (Table 8) was a plateau in vimseltinib exposures between 100 and 300 mg/kg, which was used to inform dose
selection in repeat-dose toxicology studies.

Distribution
Plasma Protein
Binding

An in vitro distribution study characterized the plasma protein binding of vimseltinib in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human plasma. Percent
bound values ranged from 95.4% in monkey, 96.4% in dogs and 99.3% in rat plasma. Percent bound in human plasma was determined to be
96.5 (DCC-3014-03-0002, eCTD 4.2.2.3).

CNS Penetrability

An in vivo distribution study to assess CNS penetrability was performed in rats. Vimseltinib exhibited a brain:plasma ratio of 0.73,
indicating a significant 73% CNS penetrability after systemic IV dosing (DCC-3014-03-0015, eCTD 4.2.2.3).

Tissue Distribution

After oral dosing to male Sprague Dawley and Long Evans rats, [14C]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity was widely distributed to
almost all tissues by the first collection time point (0.5 hours postdose). Radioactivity was cleared from most tissues by 168 hours
postdose, but still quantifiable in the meninges, eye uveal tract, eye(s), and eye vitreous humor at 672 hours(DCC-3014-03-0022,
eCTD 4.2.2.4).

Metabolism
Metabolite Profile

The metabolite profiles of [14C]-vimseltinib were evaluated in vitro using mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human hepatocytes and microsomes
(DCC-3014-03-0008 and DCC-3014-03-0023, both eCTD 4.2.2.4). Metabolism was species-dependent. [14C]-vimseltinib was metabolized in
mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human hepatocytes and microsomes via oxidation, N-dealkylation, and dehydrogenation. No Phase II metabolites
were detected. Up to 7 metabolites were tentatively identified/characterized in hepatocytes and microsomes, in addition to unchanged vimseltinib.
Metabolites detected in human hepatocytes and microsomes were also found in other species. Metabolic profiling as part of the human AME

53
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the
positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
study (DCC-3014-01-003, eCTD 5.3.3.1) determined that primary metabolism occurred by oxidation, N-demethylation, and N-dealkylation;
secondary biotransformation pathways included N-demethylation, dehydrogenation, and oxidation.
Metabolite identification studies in hepatocytes were also completed (DCC-3014-03-0008). Of the 24 identified metabolites observed across all
species (mouse, rat, dog, monkey, human), only four were present at levels high enough for UV detection in the hepatocyte incubations, and only
one (C6, DP-7005) was observed by UV in the human hepatocyte incubations. No human-specific metabolites were observed in the study, but
neither mouse rat, dog, nor monkey formed all the vimseltinib metabolites observed in human hepatocyte incubations. The rat and dog
hepatocytes between them provided coverage of all the observed human metabolites except component C19. This metabolite coverage supported
the use of rats and dogs as appropriate toxicological species.
The metabolism of [14C]-vimseltinib after a single IV bolus or oral administration was evaluated in dogs (DCC-3014-03-0021, eCTD 4.2.2.4).
Vimseltinib and DP-7005 were the major circulating components in plasma after an IV or oral dose. Vimseltinib underwent biotransformation and
was primarily metabolized via N-dealkylation to yield the major metabolite DP-7005 after both oral and IV administration (DCC-3014-03-0022,
eCTD 4.2.2.4). Minor biotransformation pathways included oxidation, dehydrogenation, and glucuronidation.
While the metabolite DP-7005 was minimally formed in rat and human hepatocytes, it was formed to a much greater extent in dog and monkey
hepatocytes (DCC-3014-03-0008). Metabolite DP-7005 was experimentally measured in the plasma of both rats and dogs after single or multiple
oral dose vimseltinib PK studies, although its appearance in rat plasma was minimal (approximately 2% compared to exposure of parent
vimseltinib) consistent with its minimal formation in microsomal preparations. The minimal appearance of DP-7005 in rat contrasts with the
proportion formed in dogs after oral administration of vimseltinib, wherein metabolite DP-7005 exposure dominated by up to approximately 5fold compared to parent vimseltinib in multiple dose studies (DCC-3014-04-0005, eCTD 4.2.3.2). Metabolite DP-7005, like vimseltinib, is a
CSF1R inhibitor (IC50 = 65 nM). In the human AME study, the metabolite-to-parent molar AUC ratio for DP-7005 was 0.05 confirming minimal
formation of DP-7005 in humans after a single oral dose (DCC-3014-01-003 CSR).
Metabolic
Stability

The overall extent of vimseltinib stability in hepatocytes from highest to lowest followed the rank order mouse§rat§dog§human>monkey. Halflives in human and mouse hepatocyte preparations were equal or greater than 4 hours, as no substrate loss was observed after 240 minutes of
incubation with mouse and human hepatocytes (DCC-3014-03-0003, eCTD 4.2.2.4).

Excretion
The excretion of [14C]-vimseltinib was evaluated after a single administration to rats via oral gavage. Most of the [14C]-vimseltinib-derived
radioactivity after oral administration to both intact males and females was excreted primarily in feces within 96 hours after dosing. Excretion
profiles were similar between males and females, suggesting no sex differences. Following oral dosing to BDC male rats, a significant percentage
of radioactivity was eliminated in the bile after oral dosing, indicating the biliary excretion was a significant route of elimination of absorbed
[14C]vimseltinib-derived radioactivity. Fecal excretion was the major pathway for elimination of [14C]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity in intact
and BDC rats after oral dosing (DCC-3014-03-0022).

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
The excretion of [14C]-vimseltinib was evaluated after a single IV or oral administration to dogs. Fecal and/or biliary excretion were the major
pathway for elimination of [14C]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity in male and female dogs (DCC-3014-03-0021).
Elimination of vimseltinib in humans occurred by metabolism and urinary and fecal excretions. In human AME study DCC-3014-01-003, when
extrapolated to infinity, 37.5% of the administered 14Cဩvimseltinib dose was excreted in urine and 42.9% in feces.
Drug-Drug Interaction
CYP Reaction
Phenotyping

In reaction phenotyping studies (time course studies in microsomal preparations and recombinant human CYP enzyme incubations; DCC-301403-0007, eCTD 4.2.2.4) vimseltinib disappearance ranged from zero to 10% over a 120 minute incubation period when human liver microsomes
were incubated with vimseltinib at various concentrations (0.1, 1 and 10 ȝM). Substrate loss was 6% or less in the absence of microsomal protein,
and no loss occurred in the absence of the NADPH-generating system at all three concentrations of vimseltinib (Module 2.6.5, Table 39).
Incubations of vimseltinib (0.1 and 1 ȝM) were performed in a panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes (20 pmol CYP/mL; 30 minutes)
(Module 2.6.5, Table 39). Loss of vimseltinib (0.1 ȝM) ranged from 2.8 to 22.3% in incubations with the panel of recombinant human CYP
enzymes. The negative control incubations, control bactosomes and reductase control, resulted in 0.6 and 3.9% substrate loss, respectively. Loss
of vimseltinib (1.0 ȝM) ranged from zero to 18.6% in incubations with the panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes. The negative control
incubations (control bactosomes and reductase control) resulted in 3.1 and 7.5% substrate loss, respectively..

CYP Induction
and Inhibition

In vitro, both vimseltinib and DP-7005 exhibited IC50 values >40 μM for all CYP isoforms studied (40 μM was the highest concentration studied
for DP-7005), and IC50 values >100 μM were exhibited by vimseltinib for five out of seven CYP isoforms (DCC-3014-03-0001, eCTD 4.2.2.4).
Additionally, neither vimseltinib nor DP-7005 exhibited time-dependent or metabolism-dependent inhibition of any CYP isoform, demonstrating
little to no risk for time- or metabolism-dependent inhibition of CYP isoforms.
Vimseltinib demonstrated low to minimal risk for CYP3A4 or CYP1A2 enzyme induction, and a slight risk for CYP2B6 induction at anticipated
clinical exposures (<10 μM; DCC-3014-03-0009, eCTD 4.2.2.4).

Transporters

Vimseltinib is a moderate to weak inhibitor of various human efflux transporters (IC50 values from 4.35 to 51.5) (DCC-3014-03-0006, eCTD
4.2.2.6). Two transporters, BCRP and OCT2, were inhibited by vimseltinib with IC50 values of less than 1 ȝM (0.556 and 0.456 ȝM,
respectively)..
The efflux ratio of vimseltinib across MDCKII-MDR1 (P-gp) cells was 2.62 and decreased to 1.48 in the presence of a P-gp inhibitor (valspodar).
Vimseltinib was not a substrate of BCRP, BSEP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3 OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE-2K. DP-7005 was also not a
substrate of MATE1 or MATE2-K.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The toxicological profile of vimseltinib has been characterized in an extensive program of
repeated-dose toxicity studies of up to 26 weeks in rats and 39 weeks in dogs, a standard battery
of genetic toxicity studies, carcinogenicity studies in transgenic mice and rats (ongoing), and
reproductive and development toxicity studies in rats and rabbits. In addition, an in vitro
assessment of the potential for vimseltinib to produce phototoxicity was performed. Pivotal
toxicology studies were conducted in compliance with current International Conference on
Harmonisation (ICH) Harmonised Tripartite Guidelines, generally accepted procedures for the
testing of pharmaceutical compounds, and in concordance with the United States FDA GLP
regulations, 21 CFR Part 58.
In repeat dose toxicity studies of up to 26 weeks in rats, there were findings of swollen head
and/or limbs, and abnormal teeth at doses of 1 mg/kg/day. The dental effects at doses of 5
mg/kg/day in male rats were associated with lower food consumption and reduced body weight.
Chronic progressive nephropathy occurred in animals receiving  2.5 mg/kg/day. Degeneration
of blood vessels in multiple tissues and increased physis thickness was observed in rats receiving
5 mg/kg/day.
In repeat dose toxicity studies of up to 39 weeks in dogs, there were findings of periocular
swelling, epiphora, excessive lacrimation and discolored and depigmented skin on the head and
legs (which were swollen and wrinkled [head only]) were observed for animals administered 4
and 8 mg/kg/day. All observations showed complete or partial recovery by the end of the
recovery phase at 4 mg/kg/day, while observations of wrinkled skin, excessive lacrimation, and
skin de-pigmentation (females only) persisted throughout the recovery phase at 8 mg/kg/day.
Vimseltinib-related increased body weight and body weight gain were noted during the dosing
phase for animals administered 8 mg/kg/day; however, during the recovery phase, animals
previously administered 8 mg/kg/day were observed with body weight loss and clinical
observations of decreased activity, thin appearance, fever, and hunched posture (which
necessitated veterinary examination and/or treatment). Microscopic findings included slight to
moderate edema in the skin collected from the head of animals administered 8 mg/kg/day, which
correlated with macroscopic observations of swollen head and subcutaneous tissue having a
gelatinous appearance. Based on the veterinary intervention and body weight loss noted during
the recovery phase for animals administered 8 mg/kg/day, this dose level was considered
adverse.

Overall, the 28-day study identified signals for inflammation, hematologic, hepatic, renal, bone,
and gastrointestinal toxicities.
In the 13-week repeat-dose rat toxicology study (Study No. 8403647-1) with a 4-week recovery
phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 1.876, 3.75, or 7.5
mg/kg/day. Findings were similar to the 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study and included
increases in AST, ALT, fibrinogen, platelets, and neutrophils and decreases in red cell mass,
calcium, phosphate, and reticulocytes. Histopathology data are tabulated in Section 19.3.
In the 26-week repeat-dose rat toxicology study (Study No. 8413878) with a 4-week recovery
phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 1, 2.5, or 5 mg/kg/day.
Findings were similar to the 28-day and 13-week repeat-dose toxicology study.
Clinical pathology findings are supportive of an inflammatory state, as well as risk for
hepatotoxicity and bone-related findings.

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

Administration of vimseltinib was associated with decreased calcium and phosphate. The
Applicant suggests that the decreased calcium is a result of reduced serum albumin levels as
most serum calcium is bound to albumin; however, the levels of calcium and phosphate return to
that of the controls during recovery while serum albumin does not.
The bone, kidney, and small intestine play a role in serum calcium and phosphate homeostasis.
Administration of vimseltinib was associated with chronic progressive nephropathy and physeal
thickening of the sternum and femur. Taken together these data suggest that the observed
decreases in calcium are unlikely due to reduced serum albumin.
In the following histopathology tables, the findings of degeneration/necrosis largely correspond
to vessel findings. Vimseltinib-related degeneration/necrosis of vessels occurred in multiple
tissues/organs of animals administered 2.5 mg/kg/day. Vessel findings were characterized by
fibrinoid necrosis of variably sized vessel (primarily artery or arteriole) walls, often with cellular
debris and/or variable numbers of inflammatory cells within the wall and perivascular
inflammatory cell infiltrates.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
Table 10

39-Week Dog Study

Study Number: DCC-3014-04-0013, eCTD 4.2.3.2
Study Title:
A 39-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study with DCC-3014 in Beagle Dogs with a 4Week Recovery Phase
Key Study Findings:
x

Vimseltinib was well tolerated during the dosing phase of 2, 4, or 8 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL for vimseltinib
was 4 mg/kg/day.

x

Beagle dogs showed periocular swelling, epiphora, excessive lacrimation and discolored and depigmented skin
on the head and legs (4 mg/kg/day). All observations showed complete or partial recovery at 4 mg/kg/day
while observations of wrinkled skin, excessive lacrimation, and skin de-pigmentation (females only) persisted
throughout the recovery phase at 8 mg/kg/day.

GLP compliance: Yes
Dose and frequency of dosing:

0, 2, 4 or 8 mg/kg/day for 39 weeks

Route of administration:
Formulation/Vehicle:

Oral gavage
0.4% (w/v) hydroxypropyl methylcellulose (80 to 120 cps) in reverse osmosis water

Species/Strain:

Dog/Beagle

Number/Sex/Group:

Main: 6/sex/group for 0, 4, and 8 mg/kg/day groups; Recovery: 3/sex for 2 mg/kg/day

Age:

5 to 6 months

Satellite groups/Unique design:

Not applicable

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
Findings
Died or Sacrificed Moribund

All animals survived to scheduled necropsy.

Body Weight and Food
Consumption

Reversible, increased body weight and body weight gain were observed in dogs
administered 8 mg/kg/day. No vimsetlinib-related alterations in food consumption
were noted at any dose level.

Ophthalmoscopy

Reversible, periocular swelling and epiphora (partially reversible) were observed in
dogs administered 8 mg/kg/day.

ECG

No significant treatment-related findings

Hematology

Hematologic findings were mild in magnitude, generally non-progressive, lacked
microscopic correlates, with reversibility during recovery.

Clinical Chemistry

Clinical chemistry findings were mild in magnitude, generally non-progressive, lacked
microscopic correlates, and exhibited evidence of reversibility during recovery.
Reversible, mildly increased AST and CK activities were observed in dogs
administered 8 mg/kg/day, which likely resulted from vimseltinib mediated inhibition
of CSF1R function in macrophages, which has been reported to promote increased
AST and CK activities without muscle injury (Radi et al., 2011; Wang et al., 2011).

Urinalysis

No significant treatment-related findings

Organ Weight

No significant treatment-related findings

Microscopic Observations

Microscopic findings included slight to moderate edema in the skin in animals
administered 8 mg/kg/day, which correlated with macroscopic observations of swollen
head and subcutaneous tissue having a gelatinous appearance.
Non-reversible, minimal to moderate mineralization of the epithelium was observed in
males administered 4 mg/kg/day. No noteworthy microscopic findings occurred in
other tissues, including the liver.

Abbreviations: AUC0-24=area under the concentration versus time curve from 0 to 24 hours; AST=aspartate; BLQ=below the
limit of quantitation; CK=creatine kinase; Cmax=maximum drug concentration; CSF1R=colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor;
NOAEL=no observed adverse effect level.

The FDA’s Assessment:
In the 28-day repeat-dose dog toxicology study (Study No. 8332977), vimseltinib was
administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 2.5, 7.5, 15, or 25 mg/kg/day. Vimseltinib-related
moribund euthanasia occurred in all dogs at 15 mg/kg (on Day 24) and 25 mg/kg (on
Day 8) during the dosing phase due to poor food consumption, significant body weight loss, and
clinical signs of toxicity. Findings in the study included:
x
x

decreases in phosphate, calcium, and APTT
increases in creatine kinase (up to 207%), AST (up to 159%), ALT (up to 40%), GLDH,
fibrinogen, and platelets

Vimseltinib-related microscopic findings included:
69
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
x
x
x

decreases in zymogen granules and acinar cell degeneration/necrosis in exocrine pancreas
atrophy, single-cell necrosis, inflammation with infiltrating neutrophils and mucosal
hemorrhage in gastrointestinal tract and associated lymphoid tissues (GALT/Peyer’s
patch)
infiltrating neutrophils in multiple tissues including liver and heart.

In the 13-week repeat-dose dog toxicology study (Study No. 8403702) with a 4-week recovery
phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 2, 4, or 8 mg/kg/day.
Findings were similar to the 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study but less severe. Clinical
pathology and histopathology data are tabulated in Section 19.3.
In the 39-week repeat-dose dog toxicology study (Study No. 8413879) with a 4-week recovery
phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 2, 4, or 8 mg/kg/day.
Findings were similar to the 13-week repeat-dose toxicology study, and there were fewer
microscopic findings in the 39-week study when compared to 4-week dog study with higher
doses. In dogs, skin findings included a gelatinous appearance, edema, and immune cell
infiltration. Dose-dependent epididymal mineralization in dogs receiving 4 mg/kg/day persisted
into recovery.

Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
The genotoxic potential for vimseltinib to induce DNA mutations, micronuclei, or unrepaired
DNA breaks has been investigated. Vimseltinib tested negative for mutagenicity in a GLP Ames
assay, but positive for in vitro micronuclei formation in the absence of metabolic activation when
incubated with human lymphocytes for 24 hours at 16 μg/mL. In accordance with ICH S2(R1),
the genotoxic potential for vimseltinib was further evaluated in vivo in rats, testing negative for
both micronuclei formation in bone marrow and unrepaired DNA breaks in the liver. Based on

Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant conducted a 26-week carcinogenicity study in 001178-T (hemizygous) RasH2
transgenic mice (DCC-3014-04-0018, eCTD 4.2.3.3.2). Male and female mice were administered
vehicle control or 1.0, 4.0, or 12.5 mg/kg/day vimseltinib via oral gavage once daily for up to 26
weeks; or positive control article (MNU) once on Day 1. Dose levels of vimseltinib evaluated in
this study were consistent with feedback on the study design provided by the Agency Executive
Carcinogenicity Advisory Committee (ECAC). Vimseltinib was well tolerated, and no
vimseltinib-related carcinogenic potential was noted for animals administered up to 12.5
mg/kg/day, the highest dose tested. This dose level corresponded to mean Cmax and AUC0-24
values of 7320 ng/mL and 102,000 h*ng/mL, respectively, for vimseltinib and 207 ng/mL and
3380 h*ng/mL, respectively, for DP-7005 (metabolite) on Day 182 of the dosing phase.
x
x
x
x
x

No vimseltinib-related mortality or effects on survival were noted. A clear carcinogenic
response was noted for positive control animals. The increased mortality for the positive
control group was attributed primarily to the presence of malignant neoplasms.
No vimseltinib-related food consumption or organ weight effects or macroscopic
observations were noted.
Vimseltinib-related clinical observations for animals administered 12.5 mg/kg/day
included a swollen dorsal cervical area and scabs on the dorsal cervical area.
Vimseltinib-related, increased body weight or body weight gain was noted for males
administered 4.0 mg/kg/day and females administered 1.0 mg/kg/day.
No vimseltinib-related effects on the occurrence or types of neoplasms were noted. No
vimseltinib-related microscopic findings, including mixed cell inflammation in meninges,
were noted in the brain of animals at any dose level. Non-neoplastic, vimseltinib-related
findings were noted in the spleen, femur, and/or stifle joint. In the spleen, minimally or
slightly increased pigmented macrophages were noted in animals administered
1.0 mg/kg/day. In the femur and/or stifle joint, minimal or slight edema was noted in
animals administered 4.0 mg/kg/day, with a higher incidence and/or severity in males.

The Applicant is conducting a 104-Week rat carcinogenicity study of vimseltinib in Sprague
(b) (4)
Dawley rats (DCC-3014-04-0025, eCTD 2.6.7 Table 21).

were observed on sperm parameters in treated males, estrous cycling in treated females, or mating and fertility in
treated males and females at any dose level.

x

Vimseltinib at a dose of 15 mg/kg/day was a selective developmental toxicant (teratogenic) in rats. Under

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. Based on data from animal studies,
vimseltinib can cause fetal harm. In Study No. DCC-3014-04-0011, after administration during
the period of fetal organogenesis, structural abnormalities occurred at 2.5 mg/kg/day
(approximately 3 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). Fetuses from
females receiving 2.5 and 5 mg/kg/day had increased incidences of small parietals and
supernumerary suture bones. Fetuses from females receiving 5 mg/kg/day also had incomplete
ossification of supraocciptals. Fetuses from females receiving 15 mg/kg/day (approximately 23
times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC) also had incomplete ossification of
multiple sites including the supraoccipital, thoracic centra, and skull. Heart and liver
malformations were also observed at 15 mg/kg/day as both organs were observed to be
misshapen.
In a fertility and early embryonic development study (Study No. DCC-3014-04-0020), male rats
were administered 1, 2.5, or 5 mg/kg/day of vimseltinib starting 10 weeks before cohabitation,
during cohabitation with untreated females, and at least 2 weeks post-cohabitation. Lower
epididymal and testes weights were observed at 5 mg/kg/day (approximately 12 times the
exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). There were no treatment-related effects on
mating, fertility, or sperm parameters at any dose tested. Female rats were administered 2.5, 5, or
10 mg/kg/day of vimseltinib 2 weeks prior to cohabitation with untreated males and during
cohabitation until gestational day 7. Although there were no treatment-related effects on mating
or estrous cycles, vimseltinib administered daily resulted in post-implantation loss at 10
mg/kg/day in female rats (approximately 20 times the exposure at the recommended dose based
on AUC).
The Applicant also performed a pre- and post-natal study (Study No. DCC-3014-04-0029) to
observe the effects of oral administration of vimseltinib from the period of implantation to
lactation. The reproductive health of the F1 generation was also assessed.
F0 female rats were treated with 0.3, 1, or 3 mg/kg/day of vimseltinib starting on gestational day
(GD) 6 until lactation day (LD) 20. A total of 6 F0 rats (1, 1, and 4 in the 0.3, 1, and 3 mg/kg/day
groups respectively) were euthanized after total litter losses between LD 0-5. F1 rats from high
dose females had reduced survival.

80

Other Toxicology Studies
5.5.5.1.

Phototoxicity

The Applicant’s Position:
In Vitro 3T3 Neutral Red Uptake Phototoxicity Assessment of Vimseltinib
An in vitro 3T3 neutral red uptake assay was conducted to assess potential phototoxicity of
vimseltinib. The relative reduction in viability of BALB/c 3T3 mouse fibroblasts exposed to
vimseltinib and ultraviolet radiation (+UVR; 5 J/cm2 of UVA and 18 or 19 or 20 mJ/cm2 of
UVB), was compared with the viability of fibroblasts exposed to vimseltinib in the absence of
ultraviolet radiation (-UVR). Relative cell viability remained unchanged across all vimseltinib
concentrations under both non-irradiation and irradiation conditions: thus, IC50 values could not
be calculated due to the lack of cytotoxicity. Vimseltinib was not phototoxic to BALB/c 3T3
cells in the presence or absence of simulated solar light.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position.

The calculated safety margins for the definitive repeat dose toxicity studies with vimseltinib
were calculated by dividing the total AUC0ဨ24 from the respective toxicology study at the
NOAEL, STD10, or HNSTD with the human total steady state AUC0ဨ24 (13,400 ng*hr/mL) dosed
at 30 mg twice weekly (Population Pharmacokinetic Modeling Report, eCTD 5.3.3.5). The
margins were based on exposures that were available at the end of the dosing period unless
otherwise noted. Margins were determined based on sex combined group mean values unless
otherwise noted. Total exposures were used given that vimseltinib is highly protein bound in
mouse, rat, dog and human plasma with fraction unbound of 2.8%, 0.65%, 3.3%, and 3.3%,
respectively.

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Clinical Pharmacology review focused on the appropriateness of the proposed dosage, use in
participants with hepatic impairment, and potential drug-drug interactions (DDI).
The Applicant’s proposed dosage of vimseltinib 30 mg orally twice weekly, with a minimum of
72 hours between doses, with or without food is generally acceptable based on the integrated
analysis of PK along with the dose- and exposure-response relationships for efficacy and safety.
The current submission included data evaluating the effect of vimseltinib on the PK and safety in
patients with mild hepatic impairment. As there was an insufficient number of patients with
moderate hepatic impairment using the classification from the National Cancer Institute’s Organ
Dysfunction Working Group (NCI-ODWG), a post-marketing requirement (PMR) was issued to
evaluate the PK and safety of vimseltinib in participants with moderate hepatic impairment
(NCI-ODWG).
In addition, vimseltinib’s potential for DDIs was inadequately addressed. Based on in vitro data,
vimseltinib was shown to inhibit BCRP, OATP1B1/OATP1B3, and OCT2, but no clinical data is
available to determine the clinical consequence of these DDIs. Therefore, three PMRs were
issued to evaluate vimseltinib with substrates of BCRP, OATP1B1/OATP1B3, and OCT2.
Recommendations
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the information submitted in NDA 219304
and recommends approval. They key review issues and recommendations are summarized in
Table 15.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical PK profile of vimseltinib was investigated in 6 clinical studies.
Three Phase 1 clinical pharmacology studies in healthy participants have been completed:
x

A PK study including evaluation of food effects (DCC-3014-01-002)

x

A human absorption, metabolism, and excretion study (DCC-3014-01-003)

x

A study to assess the potential for drug-drug interactions with a P-gp inhibitor and a
gastric acid-reducing agent (DCC-3014-01-006)

One Phase 1 clinical pharmacology study is ongoing:
x

A hepatic impairment study (DCC-3014-01-004)

Two patient studies are ongoing:
x

The supportive Phase 1/2 first-in-human study (DCC-3014-01-001) in participants
with TGCT and advanced malignant solid tumor (MST)

x

The pivotal Phase 3 study DCC-3014-03-001 (MOTION) in participants with TGCT

Population PK (PopPK) analysis of data from healthy participants and participants with TGCT
and advanced MST was conducted to characterize the PK of vimseltinib and the effects of
intrinsic and extrinsic factors on the PK of vimseltinib. Physiological-based pharmacokinetic
(PBPK) modeling and simulation were performed to assess drug-drug interaction liability of
vimseltinib as an inducer of CYP2B6, inhibitor of permeability glycoprotein (P-gp), breast
cancer resistance protein (BCRP), and organic cation transporter 2 (OCT2). Exposure-response
(E-R) analyses were conducted to investigate relationships between vimseltinib exposure and
efficacy and safety endpoints and evaluate the suitability of the investigated dosing regimen of
30 mg twice weekly.
Concentration QTc modeling was performed using data from participants with advanced MST
from the Phase 1/2 study (DCC-3014-01-001) and data from healthy participants from DCC3014-01-002.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position, except for the following:
x

Physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling and simulation was also used
to evaluate vimseltinib as an inhibitor of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

The Applicant’s Position:
The totality of evidence shows that vimseltinib 30 mg twice weekly is the optimal dosing
regimen in patients with TGCT. The efficacy, safety, and PK/PD data from the Phase 1/2 study
(DCC-3014-01-001) were evaluated to determine the recommended dose to evaluate in the
Expansion Cohorts of the Phase 1/2 Study and the Phase 3 MOTION study. The 30 mg twice
weekly dose studied in the dose escalation cohort of the Phase 1/2 study was selected based on
the manageable safety profile, PK, pharmacodynamic, and the objective responses observed.
A loading dose was not recommended as the simulated median time to steady state in
participants with TGCT receiving the 30 mg twice weekly dose of vimseltinib was similar with
or without 5 daily 30 mg vimseltinib loading doses.
The available clinical data from the pivotal MOTION study, along with efficacy and safety data
collected to date from the Phase 1/2 study, continue to support vimseltinib administration at the
recommended dose of 30 mg twice weekly with no loading dose in participants with TGCT.
In the pivotal MOTION study, vimseltinib 30 mg twice weekly demonstrated statistically
significant improvements in ORR for participants with TGCT at Week 25 compared to placebo
based on independent radiological review (IRR) per Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid
Tumors (RECIST) v1.1 (40% versus 0%; p<0.0001) and per tumor volume score (TVS) (67%
versus 0%; p<0.0001). In addition, the study met all key secondary endpoints demonstrating
statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvements in range of motion (ROM),
physical function, worst stiffness, QoL, and worst pain compared to placebo. Adverse events
observed with vimseltinib were manageable.
Maximal vimseltinib target engagement in participants with TGCT was demonstrated by
dose-dependent reduction in nonclassical monocytes (NCMs) and a steep increase in CSF1 levels
at 30 mg twice weekly and 10 mg daily doses of vimseltinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. The proposed dosage of vimseltinib 30 mg
twice weekly is supported by the available data; however, limited dose ranging and dose
optimization was performed. See FDA’s assessment on Section 6.3.2.2 for more details.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

The Applicant’s Position:
Intrinsic Factors
Hepatic Impairment
Hepatic excretion is expected to account for a substantial portion of the elimination of
vimseltinib. The potential impact of baseline hepatic function on vimseltinib exposure and safety
is currently being investigated in Study DCC-3014-01-004. The interim PK analysis of the
results from participants with mild hepatic impairment (as per Child-Pugh classification) and
their matched (by weight, sex, race and age) controls with normal hepatic function demonstrated
that following a single 10 mg vimseltinib dose:
Mild hepatic impairment resulted in 42% lower geometric mean Cmax for total vimseltinib
compared to matched participants with normal hepatic function (geometric mean AUC0inf was 24% lower, but the 90% CIs included 100%). There was no evidence of increased
total vimseltinib exposure in participants with mild hepatic impairment.
There was no evidence of increased total DP-7005 exposure in participants with mild
hepatic impairment.
Mild hepatic impairment did not affect protein binding of vimseltinib nor DP-7005.
There was no evidence of concentration-dependent protein binding.
Liver function based on National Cancer Institute classification was not identified as a covariate
in the PopPK analysis.
No dose adjustment is recommended in patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A).
The safety and efficacy of vimseltinib in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment
(Child-Pugh B and C) have not been established; evaluation of the PK of vimseltinib in patients
with moderate and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B and C) is ongoing.
Renal Impairment
Renal excretion is not expected to account for a substantial portion of the elimination of
unchanged vimseltinib. Based on the PopPK analysis, where estimated glomerular filtration rate
(eGFR) was not identified as a covariate, no relevant differences in steady state exposure are
anticipated for mild and moderate renal function (eGFR 37.7 mL/min/1.73 m2).
No dose adjustment is recommended for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment. No
clinical data is available in patients with severe renal impairment; a recommended dose of
vimseltinib has not been established in patients with severe renal impairment.
Race

89
