ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
In the PopPK analysis, an exploratory covariate-parameter relationship was identified for
participants of Black or African American race, who had a ~1.3-fold higher apparent peripheral
volume of distribution (Vp/F) compared to non-Black or African American participants.
However, the effect on steady state exposure parameters was within the 80% to 125% limits.
Participants With TGCT Versus Healthy Participants
In the PopPK analysis, healthy participants had a ~0.7-fold lower apparent central volume of
distribution (Vc/F) and a ~2.1-fold increased Vp/F compared to participants with TGCT. Healthy
participants were estimated to have a 1.13-fold higher maximum concentration during a dosing
interval at steady state (Cmax,ss) than that of the reference participant. The effect did not exceed
the 80% to 125% reference area.
Other Intrinsic Factors
In the PopPK analysis, the effect of body weight was included using allometric scaling with
fixed exponents such that participants with higher body weight had higher apparent clearance
(CL/F), Vc/F, Vp/F, and apparent inter-compartmental clearance (Q/F). Participants with lower
body weight had a higher vimseltinib exposure in terms of average concentration during a dosing
interval at steady state (Cavg,ss), Cmax,ss, and minimum concentration during a dosing interval at
steady state (Cmin,ss) compared to heavier participants. The effects of the other identified
covariates were limited and did not exceed the 80% to 125% reference area. Therefore, no dose
adjustments based on body weight is recommended.
Extrinsic Factors
Effects of Other Drugs on Vimseltinib
P-gp Inhibitors
Concomitant administration of single-dose vimseltinib with 200 mg of itraconazole (a P-gp
inhibitor) once daily showed that whilst peak exposure (Cmax) to vimseltinib was comparable
when administered alone and in the presence of itraconazole, total exposure to vimseltinib (area
under the plasma concentration curve for a specified time [AUC0-t] and area under the plasma
concentration curve from zero to infinity [AUC0-inf]) was approximately 17% to 22% higher in
the presence of itraconazole, suggesting that P-gp inhibitors have a weak effect on vimseltinib.
The coadministration of a P-gp inhibitor reduces vimseltinib CL/F by 18%. Since the change in
steady-state exposure is driven by the change in CL/F for drugs with a long half-life such as
vimseltinib, this reduction in CL/F was used in the E-R analyses to predict the proportions of
safety events following administration of vimseltinib administered with and without a P-gp
inhibitor. Following 30 mg twice weekly dosing, the proportions of safety events at Week 25
were predicted to be similar when vimseltinib was administered with and without a P-gp
inhibitor.

Proton Pump Inhibitors
Concomitant administration of vimseltinib with rabeprazole (a proton pump inhibitor) 20 mg
once daily reduced peak (Cmax) and total exposures to vimseltinib (AUC0-t and AUC0-inf) by
approximately 21% to 26% suggesting that proton pump inhibitors have a weak effect on
vimseltinib absorption.

Outstanding Issues

The Applicant’s Position:
As discussed during the PreဨNDA meeting, the Phase 1 study DCC-3014-01-004 evaluating the
effects of hepatic impairment on the PK of vimseltinib is ongoing. An interim PK analysis of the
data from participants with mild hepatic impairment was submitted in the NDA.
The PK results from the moderate and severe hepatic impairment cohorts will be submitted to the
NDA upon completion.

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
Data:
The nonclinical pharmacology and PK profile of vimseltinib was investigated by in vitro studies
with human biomaterials, including assessments of plasma protein binding, metabolism, the
potential for induction or inhibition of CYP enzymes, interactions with transporters, and hERG
channel current inhibition.
In human plasma, protein binding of vimseltinib at 10 μM was demonstrated to be 96.5%; thus,
the free fraction of vimseltinib was determined to be 3.5%. Among the animal species tested, the
percent bound values at the same concentration ranged from 95.4% in monkey to 99.3% in rat.
The bound percentage of DP-7005 (the N-des-iso-propyl active metabolite of vimseltinib) in
human plasma was 92.4%. The percent bound in animals ranged from 84.7% in monkey to
98.3% in rat.
The half-life of vimseltinib in human hepatocyte preparations was 4 hours, as no substrate loss
was observed after 240 minutes of incubation. In metabolite identification studies in human
hepatocytes, DP-7005 was the only ultraviolet-detectable metabolite. 14C-vimseltinib was
metabolized in human hepatocytes and microsomes via oxidation, N-dealkylation, and
dehydrogenation. No Phase 2 conjugated metabolites were detected. Metabolites detected in
93

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
human hepatocytes and microsomes were also found in other species. In addition to unchanged
vimseltinib, up to 7 metabolites were tentatively identified/characterized in hepatocytes and
microsomes. In human hepatocytes, >96% of the radioactivity was associated with unchanged
14
C-vimseltinib. In human microsomes, >90% of the radioactivity was associated with
unchanged 14C-vimseltinib. DP-7005 accounted for <3% radioactivity in human microsomes.
Reaction phenotyping studies in microsomal preparations and recombinant human CYP enzyme
incubations to characterize human CYP enzyme activity indicated a minor role of CYP enzymes
in the human phase 1 metabolism of vimseltinib and minimal risk for drug-drug interactions
involving vimseltinib CYP phase 1 metabolism.
In vitro, both vimseltinib and DP-7005 exhibited IC50 values >40 ȝM for all CYP isoforms
studied, and IC50 values >100 ȝM were exhibited by vimseltinib for 5 out of 7 CYP isoforms.
Additionally, neither vimseltinib nor DP-7005 exhibited time-dependent or
metabolism-dependent inhibition of any CYP isoform, demonstrating little to no risk for time- or
metabolism-dependent inhibition of CYP isoforms. Vimseltinib and DP-7005 exhibit a
low/minimal liability for a drug-drug interaction mediated by CYP inhibition.
Treatment of cultured human hepatocytes with up to 100 ȝM vimseltinib demonstrated low to
minimal risk for CYP3A4 or CYP1A2 enzyme induction and a slight risk for CYP2B6 induction
at anticipated clinical exposures, suggesting a potential risk for perpetrator drug-drug interactions
involving CYP metabolism. CYP2B6 induction was further investigated in the PBPK modeling,
which did not predict any interaction following coadministration of bupropion (a CYP2B6
substrate) with vimseltinib 30 mg twice weekly.
Nonclinical studies indicated that vimseltinib may alter the PK of concurrent medications that
are substrates for BCRP, P-gp, and OCT2.
Potential effects of vimseltinib on cardiac electrophysiology were tested using a GLP hERG
patch clamp electrophysiology assay. At concentrations of 10 ȝM and 30 ȝM, vimseltinib did not
significantly inhibit hERG channel current. In addition, vimseltinib (0.1 μM to 25 μM) exhibited
negligible binding to the hERG channel in a non-GLP in vitro binding assay.
Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics
Pharmacokinetics
The clinical pharmacology of vimseltinib was studied in the ongoing Phase 1/2 study in
participants with TGCT and advanced MST, in the ongoing MOTION study in participants with
TGCT, and in 4 Phase 1 clinical pharmacology studies (one of which is ongoing).
The Phase 1/2 study, MOTION, and Study DCC-3014-01-002 contributed data for the PopPK
analysis and the E-R analysis. Physiologically based pharmacokinetic modeling and simulations
were performed using data from Study DCC-3014-01-002, Study DCC-3014-01-003,
Study DCC-30140-01-004 (Part 1 only), and Study DCC-3014-01-006.
Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, and Excretion
94
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
Absorption
Vimseltinib was rapidly absorbed across the dose range studied in the clinical pharmacology
studies, with single doses that ranged from 6 mg to 30 mg and repeat doses that ranged from
40 mg to 50 mg once daily.
After oral administration of a single 30 mg dose of vimseltinib (given as 3 capsules each
containing 10 mg) under fasted conditions in Study DCC-3014-01-002, median time to
maximum drug concentration (Tmax) ranged from 1 to 2 hours. Cmax ranged from 305 to
433 ng/mL, and AUC0-inf ranged from 42,900 to 59,100 h•ng/mL.
In the Phase 1/2 study, after oral administration of a 30 mg dose of vimseltinib on Cycle 1 Day 1
to participants with TGCT in dose escalation, median Tmax and mean Cmax were consistent with
the results from Study DCC-3014-01-002: median Tmax ranged from 1.60 to 1.70 hours, and Cmax
ranged from 273 to 320 ng/mL.
Due to its pH dependent solubility, vimseltinib has the potential to be subject to a drug
interaction with acid-reducing agents. Study DCC-3014-01-006 evaluated the drug interaction
potential of a single dose of vimseltinib as a victim drug when co-administered with the proton
pump inhibitor rabeprazole in healthy participants. The results suggested concomitant
administration of proton pump inhibitors has a weak effect on vimseltinib absorption.
Administering vimseltinib with a high-fat meal decreased the rate of vimseltinib absorption,
possibly due to delayed gastric emptying, but there was no food effect on the extent of
absorption.
Distribution
Mild hepatic impairment did not appear to affect vimseltinib plasma protein binding as
geometric mean unbound vimseltinib fraction remained between approximately 6% and 7% in
participants regardless of hepatic function classification or concentration.
The PopPK model suggests an apparent volume of the central compartment of 88.0 L and an
apparent volume of the peripheral compartment of 28.9 L. Covariate analysis suggested that
healthy participants had a lower Vc/F, and higher Vp/F, compared to participants with TGCT.
However, the effect on steady-state exposure parameters was within the 80% to 125% limits.
Individual whole blood/plasma ratios of total radioactivity in Study DCC-3014-01-003 ranged
from 0.559 (at 10.0 hours) to 0.898 (at 0.5 hours). Based on AUC0-inf, the geometric mean whole
blood-to-plasma total radioactivity ratio was 0.751, indicating no appreciable red blood cell
partitioning.
Metabolism
Metabolic profiling as part of Study DCC-3014-01-003 determined that primary metabolism
occurred by oxidation, N-demethylation, and N-dealkylation; secondary biotransformation
pathways included N-demethylation, dehydrogenation, and oxidation. In male healthy
participants, 15 detectible metabolites were produced, 8 of which were identified. No major
95
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
circulating metabolites were detected. The most abundant, albeit minor metabolite was M3
(DP-7005), accounting for 9.67% of the dose in urine and 5.63% of the dose in feces. DP-7005,
like vimseltinib, is a CSF1R inhibitor (IC50 value of 65 nM compared to 2.8 nM for vimseltinib).
In Study DCC-3014-01-003, peak and overall exposure to the minor active DP-7005 metabolite
in plasma was appreciably lower than that observed for vimseltinib. Geometric mean molecular
weight–corrected metabolite-to-parent ratios (MPRs) for Cmax, AUC0-t, and AUC0-inf were 2.58%,
4.48%, and 5.01%, respectively. The MPRs in Study DCC-3014-01-002, Study DCC-3014-01004, and Study DCC-3014-01-006 were in a similar range.
Elimination
Elimination of vimseltinib occurred by metabolism and urinary and fecal excretions. In
Study DCC-3014-01-003, when extrapolated to infinity, 37.5% of the administered
14
C-vimseltinib dose was excreted in urine and 42.9% in feces.
Following single-dose administration of 30 mg vimseltinib to healthy participants in a fasted
condition in Study DCC-3014-01-002, geometric mean (geometric coefficient of variation) CL/F
ranged from 0.507 L/h to 0.699 L/h. Terminal elimination phase half-life (t1/2) ranged from
134 to 137 hours (39.6%). Following single-dose administration of 30 mg vimseltinib to healthy
participants in Study DCC-3014-01-006, CL/F and t1/2 were in a similar range.
In the PopPK analysis, the median effective half-life of vimseltinib based on all participants in
the analysis population was 136 hours, consistent with the t1/2 observed in Study DCC-3014-01002.
Dose Proportionality
In Study DCC-3014-01-002, dose proportionality was confirmed in healthy participants for
vimseltinib Cmax and AUC0-24h following single oral doses of vimseltinib 6 mg to 50 mg since the
slopes (ȕ1) in the linear regression for these parameters were approximately 1 and the 90%
confidence intervals (CIs) of the slope estimates included the value of 1 (Cmax: 0.9572 [0.8464,
1.0680]; AUC0-24h: 0.9343 [0.8376, 1.0309]). Additionally, vimseltinib AUC0-inf was dose
proportional between 6 mg and 30 mg; the linear regression slope (90% CIs) was 0.8804
(0.6802, 1.0805).
In the Dose Escalation Phase of the Phase 1/2 study, plasma exposure of vimseltinib following
single-dose administration of vimseltinib 20 mg to 30 mg, as measured by geometric mean
plasma AUC0-4h, AUC0-8h, and Cmax, increased with dose in participants with TGCT. Following
multiple doses, the trend for higher exposure associated with higher dose was maintained in
participants with TGCT receiving daily maintenance doses. Increased exposure for DP-7005 was
associated with higher doses.
Steady State
In the PopPK analysis, simulations based on empirical Bayes estimates of the full analysis
population showed that the 30 mg twice weekly dosing regimen of vimseltinib results in a
96
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
median accumulation ratio of 2.59. The simulated median time to steady state in participants
with TGCT receiving the 30 mg twice weekly dose was similar (28 days) with or without 5 once
daily 30 mg loading doses.
Effect of Food
Vimseltinib can be taken with or without food.
Administering vimseltinib with a high-fat meal decreased the rate of vimseltinib absorption,
possibly due to delayed gastric emptying, but there was no food effect on the extent of
absorption.
Following administration of 30 mg vimseltinib with a high-fat meal, Cmax of vimseltinib was
approximately 30% lower compared to fasted conditions (geometric LS means ratio [90% CIs]
of 69.86% [62.92%, 77.58%]), and median Tmax was delayed by 5 hours (6.0 hours compared to
1 hour under fasted conditions).
AUC0-120h, AUC0-t, and AUC0-inf for vimseltinib were comparable under fed and fasted
conditions with the ratios of geometric LS means and the corresponding 90% CIs within the
accepted 80.00% to 125.00% limits.
The PopPK analysis suggested that fed participants had an approximately 6-fold longer mean
absorption time (MAT) than fasted participants; however, this had a limited effect on steady state
exposure metrics.
Effect on QT Interval
A concentration QTc modeling analysis was performed based on the data from participants with
MST in the Phase 1/2 study and healthy participants from Study DCC-3014-01-002 (who are
physiologically similar to participants with TGCT). The results from the cardiodynamic
evaluation of the 40 mg and 50 mg doses demonstrated that vimseltinib has no clinically relevant
effect on heart rate or on cardiac conduction (the PR and QRS intervals). There was no clinically
meaningful effect on the QTc interval, and an effect on ¨¨QTcF exceeding 10 ms can be
excluded within the observed ranges of plasma concentrations of vimseltinib, up to 1774 ng/mL.
Exposure-Response Analyses
Data from the Phase 1/2 study and MOTION were used to inform the E-R model development
and analysis.
The exposure-response relationships between vimseltinib Cavg,ss and ORR per RECIST v1.1 and
per TVS at Week 25 in participants with TGCT from MOTION was well characterized by a
logistic regression model. An exposure-efficacy analysis based on data from participants with
TGCT did not show association between vimseltinib exposure and ORR per RECIST v1.1 or
TVS. A flat exposure-efficacy relationship was identified across the exposure range for the
evaluated dosing regimen.

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
The exposure-safety relationships between vimseltinib Cmax,ss and the time to first event of
periorbital edema, rash, AST elevation, and alanine aminotransferase (ALT) elevation in patients
with TGCT or MST from the Phase 1/2 study and MOTION were well characterized by Cox
proportional hazards models. For all safety endpoints, the event incidence was estimated to
increase with increasing Cmax,ss, with an initial increase for low Cmax,ss, followed by a plateau.
The impact of vimseltinib exposure was more pronounced for AST elevation events, while the
relationship was relatively flat for the other endpoints. For periorbital edema, rash, and ALT
elevation, the difference in the median predicted proportions of these safety events at Week 25
was marginal at <5% following 20 mg twice weekly, compared to 30 mg twice weekly. For AST
elevation, the difference was -7.3%.
Pharmacodynamics
The pharmacodynamic effect of vimseltinib on NCMs (circulating monocytes in peripheral
blood, which express CSF1R and are sensitive to anti-CSF1/CSF1R targeted therapies), and
plasma CSF1 levels were investigated as biomarkers to study vimseltinib target engagement.
Changes in pharmacodynamics biomarkers were assessed among the participants with TGCT
and MST from the safety population in the Phase 1/2 study. Specifically, the percentage change
from baseline in NCM and fold changes in the levels of CSF1 in plasma were summarized by
visits across Dose Escalation cohorts up to Week 25.
Vimseltinib demonstrated a dose-dependent decrease in the levels of circulating NCM in Dose
Escalation cohorts who received 10 mg to 40 mg twice weekly doses and 6 mg to 20 mg daily
doses. The mean reduction in NCM levels from baseline at Cycle 2 Day 1 in participants with
TGCT was in the range of 65% to 80%. The maximum NCM reduction was reached by Cycle 2
Day 1 (Week 5) for most cohorts and was maintained throughout the dosing period.
Increased levels of CSF1 in plasma were observed across all cohorts. A steep increase in mean
fold changes over baseline CSF1 levels at Cycle 2 Day 1 was observed from 20 mg twice weekly
dose to 30 mg twice weekly, or from 6 mg daily dose to 10 mg daily dose. The fold changes in
CSF1 levels in participants with TGCT were in a similar range, except for the lower dose cohort
(6 mg daily dose). Similar to the NCM changes, the maximum fold changes in CSF1 levels
across the cohorts were reached by Cycle 2 Day 1.
The Applicant’s Position:
The PK and PD characteristics of vimseltinib, together with the E-R analyses, support the
proposed administration of vimseltinib at 30 mg twice weekly in patients with TGCT.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
6.3.2.1.
Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence
of effectiveness?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. Clinical studies evaluating vimseltinib 30 mg twice weekly in participants with TGCT have
demonstrated strong clinical benefit and manageable safety profile, with PK/PD and E-R data
supporting the recommended dose (see Section 6.2.1 and 6.2.2 of this Assessment Aid).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that the completed clinical studies provide supportive evidence of effectiveness of
vimseltinib in the proposed patient population. See Section 6.2.
6.3.2.2.
Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. The recommended dosing regimen of vimseltinib in patients aged 18 years and older is
30 mg twice weekly at least 3 days apart with or without food. If a vimseltinib dose is missed by
more than 2 days, the patient should skip the missed dose and return to the regular dosing
schedule. This regimen is supported by its pharmacological properties and by clinical efficacy
and safety data generated to date.
Vimseltinib dose may be temporarily withheld if the recommended dose is not tolerated.
Vimseltinib may be resumed on a reduced dose as shown in the table below when the clinical
condition improves. Vimseltinib should be discontinued in patients unable to tolerate a 14 mg
dose.

Based on Table 20, if doses were administered 24 hours apart, such increase in Cmax does not
appear to be clinically meaningful (i.e., 14%). Therefore, missing the dose by 48 hours is also
not anticipated to significantly impact Cmax. A missed dose of >48 hours was not considered,
since the minimum dosing interval was 72 hours apart. Taking two doses within 24 hours would
increase the Cmax by 2-fold, based on PK linearity, and would be expected to worsen the safety.
Therefore, FDA recommends the following for missed doses: If a dose is missed by 48 hours or
less, take the missed dose as soon as possible and take the next dose on its regularly scheduled
day. If a dose is missed by more than 48 hours, skip the missed dose, and take the next dose on
its regularly scheduled day.

Vomiting
FDA recommends the following for vomiting: If vomiting occurs within 30 minutes of taking a
dose, repeat that dose. Otherwise, take the next dose on its regularly scheduled day.
Tmax was evaluated to determine recommendations for vomiting. As the Tmax ranged between 0.5
to 4 hours, 30 minutes was selected, as this was on the lower range of Tmax, which would avoid
potentially duplicating dose administration and increasing toxicity.

Administering vimseltinib with a high-fat meal decreased the rate of vimseltinib absorption,
possibly due to delayed gastric emptying, but there was no food effect on the extent of

Following administration of 30 mg vimseltinib with a high-fat meal, Cmax of vimseltinib was
approximately 30% lower compared with fasted conditions (geometric LS Means ratio
[90% CIs] of 69.86% [62.92%, 77.58%]) and median Tmax was delayed by 5 hours (6.0 hours
compared with 1.0 hour under fasted conditions).

conditions with the ratios of geometric LS means and the corresponding 90% CIs within the
accepted 80% to 125% limits.

Effect of Other Drugs on Vimseltinib
CYP Inhibitors and Inducers
Vimseltinib is metabolically stable in human microsomes and recombinant CYP enzymes,
indicating a minor role of CYP enzymes in the human phase 1 metabolism of vimseltinib and
minimal risk for victim drug-drug-interactions involving CYP phase 1 metabolism.
Inhibitors of Transporters
Nonclinical data indicated that vimseltinib is a weak substrate of P-gp and exhibits low potential
to be a victim of a drug-drug interaction mediated by P-gp inhibitors. Study DCC-3014-01-006
evaluated the drug interaction potential of a single dose of vimseltinib as a victim drug when
co-administered with a strong P-gp inhibitor, itraconazole, in healthy participants.
Concomitant administration of itraconazole 200 mg once daily showed that while peak exposure
(Cmax) to vimseltinib was comparable, total exposure (AUC0-t and AUC0-inf) was approximately
17% to 22% higher, suggesting that P-gp inhibitors have a weak effect on vimseltinib.
The co-administration of a P-gp inhibitor reduces vimseltinib CL/F by 18%. Since the change in
steady-state exposure is driven by the change in CL/F for drugs with a long half-life, such as
vimseltinib, this reduction in CL/F was used in the E-R analyses to predict the proportions of
safety events following administration of vimseltinib administered with and without a P-gp
inhibitor. Following 30 mg twice-weekly dosing, the proportions of safety events at Week 25
were predicted to be similar when vimseltinib was administered with and without a P-gp
inhibitor.
Gastric Acid-reducing Agents
Due to its pH-dependent solubility, vimseltinib has the potential to be subject to a drug
interaction with acid-reducing agents. Study DCC-3014-01-006 evaluated the drug interaction
potential of a single dose of vimseltinib as a victim drug when co-administered with the proton
pump inhibitor rabeprazole in healthy participants.
Concomitant administration of rabeprazole 20 mg once daily reduced peak (Cmax) and total
exposures (AUC0-t and AUC0-inf) to vimseltinib by approximately 21% to 26%, suggesting that
proton pump inhibitors have a weak effect on vimseltinib absorption.
Effect of Vimseltinib on Other Drugs
CYP Induction
Vimseltinib demonstrated low to minimal risk for CYP3A4 or CYP1A2 enzyme induction
during in vitro studies with human hepatocytes. A slight risk was demonstrated for CYP2B6

The FDA’s Assessment:
Food Effect: FDA agrees that food does not have a clinically significant impact on vimseltinib
PK and can be taken without regards to meals. See Section 6.3.1 for more details.
DDI and PBPK: FDA is recommending a 4-hour separation between vimseltinib and P-gp
substrates, based on PBPK risk assessment. FDA also disagrees with the Applicant’s assessment
of DDIs on CYP3A induction in vitro, inhibition of transporters, and the PBPK modeling on
transporters. See Section 6.2.2.2 and Section 19.4.3 for more details.

The Applicant’s Position:
Table 21 provides a listing of clinical studies relevant to this application.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The MOTION study was the basis for efficacy and safety (Section 6 of the USPI for vimseltinib)
approval of vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for which
surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe morbidity was
based on the results from Week 25 of Part 1 (double-blind portion). The review was based on the
123 patients who were randomized to receive vimseltinib (n=83) or placebo (n=40). One patient
in the placebo arm was not eligible for efficacy evaluation as the patient discontinued treatment
within one month after providing consent. All patients received at least one dose of vimseltinib
30 mg.
The pooled safety population (n=253) to support Sections 6 of product labeling reflects exposure
to vimseltinib in 83 patients with TGCT enrolled in the double-blind portion and in 35 patients in
the open-label portion who crossed over to vimseltinib in MOTION, and in 135 patients with
TGCT or solid tumors in other clinical trials. This safety pool contributed to the evaluation of the
incidence of any adverse event and any serious adverse events that may have occurred at a low
incidence.
As part of the review strategy, FDA conducted an independent review of efficacy data for
MOTION. The results from the supportive trial, DCC-3014-01-001, were reviewed for safety;
however, the data for efficacy were not independently verified by the review team. For the
purposes of safety evaluation regarding hepatotoxicity, FDA evaluated n=253patients were
treated with at least one dose of vimseltinib. Patients enrolled in the pharmacologic studies DCC3014-01-002, 01-003, 01-004, and 01-006 which enrolled healthy volunteers who received a
single-dose of vimseltinib were not included in the pooled safety population (n=253)

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
DCC-3014-03-001 (MOTION)
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
This is a pivotal Phase 3, placebo-controlled study of vimseltinib in patients with TGCT,
consisting of 2 parts: Part 1 was double blind and Part 2 was open label.
The double-blind and randomized design of this study allowed for adequate assessment
of vimseltinib compared to a control group. The selection of placebo as the control group
was justified because in most participating countries there were no approved systemic,
nonsurgical therapies for the treatment of TGCT in patients not amenable to surgery.
While pexidartinib is approved in the United States, it is associated with a risk of
potentially fatal liver injury and available under restricted access (REMS program)
(TURALIO, USPI 2023).
The study design is outlined in Figure 8Figure 8. The study consisted of a 42-day
screening period prior to the first dose of study drug, a Part 1 double-blind treatment
period of 24 weeks (referred to in 28-day cycles) and a Part 2 open label period until
Week 49.
Participants were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive either vimseltinib at the dose of 30
mg twice weekly or placebo for 24 weeks. Interactive response technology was used to
randomize and assign study drug. Randomization was stratified for tumor location (lower
limb/all other) and region (U.S./non-U.S.).
After completion of the End of Part 1 assessments, participants who wished to continue
to the open-label part of this study (Part 2) were unblinded. Participants randomized to
placebo crossed over to receive vimseltinib and participants initially randomized to
vimseltinib continued to receive vimseltinib in Part 2.

Sponsor’s right to terminate the study.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study design. The dose studied in MOTION
was 30 mg orally taken twice weekly. Patients were allowed to undergo surgical resection only
after completion of Part 1.
Eligibility Criteria
The Applicant’s Description:
Symptomatic patients 18 years of age with histologically confirmed TGCT for whom surgical
resection could potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe morbidity were
eligible. Patients who previously received anti-CSF1/CSF1R therapy (except for imatinib or
nilotinib) were excluded.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant highlighted a few eligibility criteria which the review team agrees with; however,
the review team included the following additional key eligibility criteria:

Patients must have symptomatic disease in a joint where ROM can be assessed.
Patients must have symptomatic disease with at least mild or moderate pain or stiffness.
Patients must have bilirubin, AST and ALT levels  upper limit of normal (ULN).
Patients must have disease that is likely to worsen, or result in significant morbidity, if
surgery is performed. A surgical team review prior to enrollment was required for all
patients to provide specific rationale for why surgery was likely to worsen morbidity.
If a patient requires analgesic, analgesic use must be stable. Patients must complete 14
consecutive days of questionnaires during the screening period and be able to complete
PRO via an electronic device.
Patients may have been pretreated with systemic therapy such as imatinib or nilotinib and
may not have been previously treated with systemic therapy that targets CSF1 or CSF1R.
Patients with active liver or biliary disease including nonalcoholic steatohepatitis
(NASH) or cirrhosis were excluded.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

The FDA’s Assessment:
TGCT is a slow-growing progressive and debilitating but non-lethal disease. ORR may
be used to measure the reduction in tumor burden over time. Due to the slow growing
nature of the tumor and the symptomatic and functional impacts of the tumor location,
supportive evidence of clinical benefit based on clinical outcome assessments (COAs)
that can evaluate improvement in pain, range of motion, and physical function is useful to
understand the drug effect.
FDA agrees with Applicant’s provided list of study endpoints. A detailed description of
the primary and key secondary endpoints are provided below.
ORR by RECIST and Tumor Volume Score (TVS):
Best overall response of complete or partial response at Week-25 per RECIST 1.1 (as
primary) and TVS (as key secondary) as assessed by blinded independent central review
were included in this study. TVS is a semi-quantitative MRI scoring system that
describes tumor mass and is based on 10% increments of the estimated volume of the
maximally distended synovial cavity or tendon sheath involved.
Active ROM:
ROM is designed to measure movement of joints in unit of degrees. Active and passive
ROM was assessed using a goniometer. Active is defined as measuring a motion
produced by the participant’s voluntary, unassisted muscle contraction, whereas passive
is defined as measuring a motion produced by the participant of external force by an
examiner during assessments.
There were two forms used to collect ROM data: 1) screening through the end of Part 1
visit (Week 25), which collected both affected and non-affected joint data, and 2) from
the end of Part 1 visit though the rest of the trial, which only collected data on the
affected joint. The data collected included the joint, side of body, motions assessed, and
degrees measured (the start and end position of motion) for active and passive motion.
For each specific joint, the relevant planes of measurement were delineated on the case
report form. ROM assessments were conducted at screening, Cycle 4, end of Part 1 visit
(Week-25), and end of treatment (EOT).
120
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
The measurement (in degrees) of the affected joint was used to derive a relative ROM
obtained through normalization to the measurement from a reference standard value
provided by the American Medical Association (AMA) per motion and type (active or
passive) (Gerhardt, 2002).At Screening, the motion with the smallest relative ROM value
(worst) was identified, and this motion was used for evaluating the change in relative
ROM. Only the motion with the most impaired ROM at screening was selected for
subsequent analyses. If there were ties, the multiple motions with the same relative ROM
value at screening were identified, and the average of the relative ROM values were
calculated at each post-Screening visit and used in the analysis as the single relative
ROM value for that specific visit. The main analysis of relative ROM was based on the
active measurement relative to a reference standard.
Change from baseline to Week-25 in active ROM was included as a key secondary
endpoint.
PROMIS-PF:
PROMIS Physical Function PROMIS-PF) is an item bank that consists of patient
reported outcome (PRO) instrument designed to assess patients’ perspectives on their
physical functioning, including functioning based on use of one’s upper extremities
(dexterity), lower extremities (walking or mobility), and central regions (neck, back), as
well as instrumental activities of daily living (IADLs). For MOTION, the Applicant
selected 15 items to measure tumor location-specific physical function (referred to as
upper and lower extremity scales). The lower extremity scale includes 13 items and is
intended for use by participants with lower extremity tumors (e.g., knee, ankle, hip, etc.).
The upper extremity scale includes 11 items and is intended for use by participants with
upper extremity tumors (e.g., digits, hand, elbow, etc.). Nine items overlap between the
two scales. Each item uses a 5-point verbal rating scale ranging from 1 (“Unable to do”)
to 5 (“Without any difficulty”). The recall period is unspecified. The PROMIS Physical
items were administered electronically (via Clinical Ink Engage mobile application) at
Week 1 Day 7 during screening period, Week 3 Day 1 during Cycle 1, Week 1 Day 1 and
Week 2 Day 6 during Cycle 2 through Cycle 6, and end of Part 1.
The table below shows the subset of 15 questions from PROMIS-PF item bank that were
selected for inclusion in the assessment of functioning of upper and lower extremities as
it related to tumor location.

The 15-item PROMIS® Physical Function measure generates a total score that ranges
from 0 to 100, where higher scores indicate better physical function. This score uses a Tscore metric with a mean of 50 and standard deviation (SD) of 10 for a relevant reference
population (i.e., U.S. general population).
According to the Applicant, the scoring of the PROMIS Physical Function score used
item-level calibrations via the Assessment Center Application Programming Interface
(API), and no item level imputation was to be made for the PROMIS Physical Function
scores based on the response pattern scoring.
If a participant answered at least half of the questions for their tumor location, then the Tscore generated based on the API scoring manual was used for analysis and those
questions with missing responses were ignored for scoring. If a participant answered
fewer than half of the questions for their tumor location, then the T-score was calculated
and presented in listings but was not used for analysis.
Brief Pain Inventory Short Form (BPI-SF):
Item 3 of the BPI-SF (Worst Pain NRS) is a single item in the 9-item BPI-SF, a PRO
instrument. BPI-SF Item 3 is designed to assess pain intensity at its worst. This item is
rated using an 11-point numeric rating scale ranging from 0 (“No pain”) to 10 (“Pain as
bad as you can imagine”). The recall period is the previous 24 hours. The BPI-SF was
administered on Day1 through Day 7 at Week 1 and Week 2 during screening; Day 2
through Day 7 at Week 1; Day 1 through Day 6 at Week 2; Day 1, Day 3 through Day 7
122
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
at Week 3 during Cycle 1; Day 1 through Day 7 at Week 1 through Week 3 during Cycle
2-6; and End of Part 1.
BPI-SF Item 3 generates a single item score ranging from 0 to 10, where higher scores
indicate greater pain intensity.
A responder analysis based on the BPI Worst Pain NRS item and analgesic use was
performed as primary analysis (referred to as BPI-30 response). The change in mean BPI
Worst Pain NRS for responder assessment was assessed by comparing data collected
during a 14-day period prior to the current visit with baseline values collected prior to the
first dose of study drug. A minimum of 4 measurements out of the 14 days was required
to compute the mean; otherwise, it was set to missing. A responder (BPI-30 response)
was defined as a patient who:
(i) experienced a decrease of at least 30% in the mean BPI Worst Pain NRS item
and
(ii) did not experience a 30% or greater increase in narcotic analgesic use.
Worst-Stiffness and EQ-5D-5L VAS:
FDA focused its review of COA data on the ROM, PROMIS-PF, and BPI-SF
information. The PRO data supporting EQ-5D-5L VAS and Worst Stiffness NRS
endpoints of were not reviewed by FDA due to the following reasons:
1. FDA did not agree with the scoring algorithm used for the Worst Stiffness NRS
endpoint. FDA recommended the Applicant to use the PRO data collected during
the 14-consecutive-day period that is closest to C1D1. However, this approach
was not used for MOTION and the Worst Stiffness was measured every alternate
day starting from Cycle-1.
2. The EQ-5D-5L VAS is a generic preference-based measure intended to provide
health utility index values for use in economic analyses and lacks evidence of
content validity for use in estimating clinical benefit.

of participants who were randomized to a study treatment regimen.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the statistical analysis plan for MOTION.
Additional details regarding the statistical analysis plan in MOTION are provided below.
Sample size assumptions:
In this study, a total of 120 patients were planned to be randomized in a 2:1 ratio to
vimseltinib and placebo arms. This sample size would provide 98% power to detect a
statistically significant difference between treatment groups, assuming true ORRs of 35%
and 5% in the vimseltinib arm and the placebo arm, respectively, using a two-sided
Fisher’s exact test at 5% type I error rate level.
Analysis Methods:
The assessments schedules and analysis methods for the primary and key secondary
endpoints are listed below. Key secondary endpoints were tested hierarchically in the
order listed in the table below to adjust for multiplicity in the hypothesis tests. For the
key secondary endpoints that are based on change from baseline assessments, mixed
model for repeated measurements (MMRM) with the sandwich estimator to estimate the
variance-covariance matrix was used. Each of these models included fixed effects for
treatment group, timepoint, treatment group by timepoint interaction, stratification factors
(region and tumor location), and the baseline value of the corresponding endpoint. The
different timepoints that were included in the model specific to each endpoint are
included in the below table.
Table 23:

Protocol Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The original protocol was approved on 15 Dec 2020. The protocol underwent 3 global
amendments and 4 regional amendments. Modifications did not impact the integrity of
the study or interpretation of the results.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA outlines the key protocol amendments below. All amendments were activated.
1. Protocol Amendment 1, dated February 11, 2021:
Applicant responded to FDA comments from the EOP meeting January 12, 2021.
a. Protocol updated to ensure case report forms specify the reason why surgical
resection would lead to functional morbidity or impairment.
b. Schedule of assessments clarified to note frequency of liver function assessment.
c. Minor modifications were made regarding the PRO analyses.
2. Amendment 2, March 7, 2022:
Applicant updated protocol to address regulatory concerns.
a.
b.
c.
d.
e.

Applicant removed requirement for serum creatinine 1.5 ULN.
Applicant added dose interruption guidance regarding cardiotoxicity risk.
Protocol updated to prohibit medications that are OCT2 substrates.
Protocol updated to add prophylactic measures for photosensitivity.
Safety monitoring procedures for Part 1 and Part 2 crossover were aligned.

3. Amendment 3, May 31, 2023:
No patients were enrolled under Protocol Amendment 3 because enrollment was
completed. Applicant updated protocol to move ORR per RECIST to secondary
endpoint and categorize secondary endpoints into hierarchical (Key-Secondary) and
non-hierarchical (Other secondary endpoints). Additionally, the Applicant updated the
protocol in response to requests from regulatory authorities and the study’s safety data

the major regulatory authorities, and in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki.

The MOTION study states that the trial was conducted in accordance with the
International Council for Harmonization Good Clinical Practice guidelines, the ethical
principles stated in the Declaration of Helsinki and applicable laws and regulations.

All investigators have been researched for conflicts of interest. None were identified as
having financial interests/arrangements with the Applicant.

The Applicant submitted a Financial Interests and Arrangements of Clinical Investigators

arrangements with the clinical investigators.
For MOTION there were a total of 261 investigators at clinical sites that enrolled study
patients. Signed financial disclosures could not be located for 5 sub-investigators for
MOTION.
Financial forms for the 256 investigators from MOTION showed no disclosable financial
interests or arrangements.

common reason for treatment discontinuation was patient withdrawal. Reasons for patient
withdrawal from the study appeared well balanced, except for withdrawal due to an
adverse reaction in the vimseltinib arm. No patient in the placebo arm withdrew due to an
AE.

The Applicant’s Position:
There were more IPDs for the placebo treatment arm than the vimseltinib arm. The
incidences of major protocol deviations were similar between the treatment groups.
FDA’s Assessment:
The incidence of important protocol deviations (1.2%, 13%) was lower in the vimseltinib
arm than in the placebo arm, respectively and the incidences of major protocol deviations
were similar (76%, 85%) respectively, in both arms. In the vimseltinib treatment arm,
there was one important protocol deviation, where a single patient did not log their doses
of vimseltinib during Cycle 2 and did not receive 7 doses of vimseltinib during Cycle 4.
In the placebo arm, there were no instances of patients in the treatment arm receiving
wrong doses or taking inappropriate other medications. In the placebo arm, 3 patients
received inappropriate other medications while enrolled, and one patient took improper
doses of placebo during Cycle 1. It is unlikely that protocol deviations had a significant
impact on efficacy analyses.

The Applicant’s Position:
In general, demographic and baseline characteristics were similar between treatment
arms. There were more White participants in the vimseltinib arm (59 participants
[71.1%]) than the placebo arm (21 participants [52.5%], respectively)
The characteristics of participants with TGCT in MOTION were representative of the
target patient population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA has independently verified the demographics information provided here and agrees
with Applicant’s position except for tumor location based on IRT where FDA noted a
discrepancy on how the tumor location was coded based on the joint type reported. As
per FDA analysis, some inconsistencies were found on how some of the joints, such as
hip, knee, and ankle, were mistakenly coded into “All other” category instead of “Lower
Limb” category. A cross tabulation of the joint type and the tumor location (per IRT)
variable are provided in the table below, highlighting the inconsistencies.

Given that this is one of the stratification factors included in the primary analysis, all the
analyses were performed using the rederived tumor location to assess the impact of
misclassification. The analysis results remained unchanged with the use of the rederived
tumor location variable.
Some countries do not collect data on race and ethnicity; therefore, the results for these
categories report high percentages of patients for whom these have not been captured.
Data on racial distribution of TGCT is scarce in the literature. One study conducted in 83
patients with TGCT who received pexidartinib, showed that 65% of patients were White,
18% per Other. 8% were Black, and 1.2% were missing. There were no Native
Americans or Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander reported (Lin 2024).
There were a total of 11% of patients enrolled in the vimseltinib arm and 10% of patients
enrolled in the placebo arm in the US. In Asia, there was only one (2.5%) patient enrolled
in the placebo arm. In Europe, which included sites in France, Italy, Netherlands,
Germany, Great Britian, Spain, Norway, Poland, and Switzerland, 69% of patients were
enrolled in the vimsletinib placebo arm and 80% enrolled in the placebo arm. Lastly,
Australia enrolled 15% and 5%, in the vimseltinib arm and placebo arm, respectively.
The ORR per subgroup analysis (US vs. other) is displayed in Figure 11 below.

The Applicant’s Position:
TGCT history was similar between treatment arms and is anticipated to be representative
of the intended population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA has independently analyzed these data and agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.
Localized TGCT is more common than diffuse TGCT and the knee is the most common
joint affected in patients with TGCT. While localized TGCT is more common, patients
with diffuse TGCT are more likely to experience recurrence (Stacchiotti 2023). Thus,
patients with localized TGCT are less likely to be eligible to receive vimseltinib.
In MOTION, diffuse TGCT was more common in both the treatment arm (69%) and the
placebo arm (70%), and joints affected were evenly distributed between treatment and
placebo arms.

ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Data:
Treatment Compliance
Mean (standard deviation [STD]) relative dose intensity (RDI) in the double-blind period
was 81.25% (18.207%) for vimseltinib and 91.62% (11.226%) for placebo. Median (min,
max) RDI in the double-blind period was 87.5% (14.6%, 102.1%) for vimseltinib and
95.8% (50.0%, 100.0%) for placebo.
The median RDI for the on-study vimseltinib treatment period, including both doubleblind and open-label, was 81.3%.
Prior and Concomitant Procedures, Medications, and Therapies
The majority of participants (74.0%) reported at least 1 prior TGCT surgery or procedure.
In addition, 22.8% reported receiving a prior TGCT systemic therapy, which included
imatinib (18.7%) and nilotinib (4.9%), and 8.9% reported prior TGCT radiation therapy.
The majority of participants received at least 1 prior or concomitant medication.
Common prior medications included paracetamol (25.4%) and ibuprofen (18.9%).
Common concomitant medications in the double-blind period included paracetamol
(43.4%) and ibuprofen (28.7%).
Rescue Medication Use
Not applicable.
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment compliance and RDI were similar between the vimseltinib and placebo arms.
Prior TGCT surgeries and procedures were as expected for the study population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Prior TGCT surgeries or procedures were completed in 64 patients (77%) in the
vimseltinib arm and 27 patients (68%) in the placebo arm. This is consistent with
expected disease history in the study population. Prior systemic treatments (with no
patients receiving prior treatment with CSF1R directed therapy) was similar in both
groups.

The analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint in Study MOTION at Week 25
demonstrated a statistically significant difference in ORR between vimseltinib and
placebo arms (Table 28 and Figure 10Figure 10).
The ORR was 40% (95% CI: 29%, 51%) in the vimseltinib arm compared with no
responses (95% CI: 0%, 9%) in the placebo arm (p<0.0001). The median DOR was not
reached; DOR ranged from 2.5+ to 19.4+ months.

Results from the sensitivity analysis in the ITT Set also demonstrated a statistically
significant difference in ORR per RECIST v1.1 up to Week 25 by blinded IRR between
vimseltinib and placebo. The ORR was 42.2% (95% CI: 31.4%, 53.5%) for the
vimseltinib arm and 0% (95% CI: 0%, 8.8%) for the placebo arm.
Vimseltinib was associated with higher response rates per RECIST v1.1 across all
subgroups in an analysis of treatment difference in ORR per RECIST v1.1 at Week 25
based on IRR by subgroup.

The study met its primary endpoint of ORR at Week 25 per RECIST v1.1 based on IRR.

adequate sample size were consistent with those from primary endpoint analyses.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of the primary endpoint results for ORR and
the Applicant’s position regarding the demonstration of statistically significant ORR
difference between the arms. FDA also considers the difference in ORR to be clinically
meaningful and to represent an acceptable primary endpoint in this context.

Missing data sensitivity analysis of change from baseline in active ROM per American

compared to those receiving placebo, regardless of the true underlying missing data
mechanism (Table 32).

The Applicant’s Position:
All key secondary endpoints assessed at Week 25 were met and were statistically significant. All
key secondary endpoints demonstrated clinically meaningful improvement from baseline at
Week 25. Results from subgroup analyses with adequate sample size were consistent with those
from key secondary endpoint analyses.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA’s assessment of each of the primary and key secondary endpoints are provided below.
ORR at Week-25 by RECIST 1.1:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s interpretation of the clinically and statistically significant
difference between the treatment arms with respect to the ORR by RECIST 1.1 at Week 25.
ORR at Week-25 by TVS:
The analysis of ORR per TVS demonstrated a statistically significant difference in ORR per TVS
between vimseltinib and placebo arms. This endpoint has not been validated as a surrogate for
any of the established endpoints for clinical benefit claims in oncology, and therefore, FDA
considered the results of the analysis of ORR by TVS as supportive of the anti-tumor activity
with vimseltinib. The FDA review team thus included a sentence describing these results in
Section 14 of product labeling, albeit after the table presenting the key efficacy results of
MOTION.

EQ-5D-5L VAS and Worst Stiffness NRS are not included in this assessment as described

These sensitivity analyses support the primary analysis conclusions. The delta-adjusting
pattern mixture model (PMM) analysis showed that the required penalty for loss of
statistical significance was an average worsening of 24% points for patients with missing
data in the vimseltinib arm. FDA concludes that the tipping point of 24% to be an
extreme scenario and is unlikely.
Some patients who were non-responders (as measured by RECIST) to vimseltinib also
had improved ROM, and some patients who received placebo also had improved ROM.
This points to an incomplete overlap between the ORR measured radiographically and
the demonstrated change in ROM. This may be due to several factors, including a
placebo effect of being on a perceived effective therapy. However, these incongruous
results (i.e., discordance on the vimseltinib arm between RECIST response and ORR, and
improved ROM on the placebo arm in the absence of response) were fairly uncommon on
both arms overall and do not overall jeopardize reliability/interpretability of ROM results.

The stratified CMH test was used to compare BPI-30 response at Week 25 between the 2
treatment arms. A statistically significant improvement in BPI-30 response rates was
observed with a difference of 26.2% (95% CI: 9.5, 42.8; p=0.0056).
As part of the sensitivity analysis for BPI-30, the following aspects were assessed:
(i) Impact of analgesic use
Overall, only <5% patients (of n=123 patients enrolled on both arms) had an
increase in narcotic analgesic use. The BPI-30 response rates remained the same
irrespective of the amount of analgesic use.
(ii) Assessment period of 14 days
The average worst pain on day-1 of each cycle was calculated using the pain
assessments obtained during the Weeks-2 and 3 of the prior cycle. At least 4 out
of this 14-day assessment period were required to compute the average worst pain
value. This 14-day assessment period and the minimum number of assessments (4
out of 14) has been further evaluated to assess the impact of these criteria on BPI30 response rate results. Two sensitivity analyses were performed by considering.
 at least 4 assessments during only Week-3 (SA-1)
 at least 4 assessments in Week-2 and at least 4 assessments during Week-3
(SA-2).
Both the sensitivity analyses results did show a difference between the arms in
terms of BPI-Response rates.
(iii)Impact of 30% change defined as part of responder definition:

analysis of BPI-worst pain using the original values as recorded. The section
below discusses the results based on these analyses.

results, the magnitude of difference between the arms was similar to that of the primary
analysis results.

BPI Worst Pain (change from baseline to Week 25 based on the original reported values)
was further assessed using the MMRM model. LS mean difference of -1.6 (95% CI: -2.3,
-0.8) for BPI worst pain NRS was observed at Week 25.
From a clinical perspective, BPI assesses the severity of pain and its impact on daily
functioning. It measures the impact of pain on daily function, location of pain, pain
medications, and amount of pain relief. Pain relief is an important component of
improvement in quality of life for patients with TGCT. The results of the BPI-30
demonstrate a clinically meaningful impact in improvement in pain in patients who
received vimseltinib over placebo. This is included in product labeling.
Overall, the 15-item PROMIS measure and BPI Worst Pain results are supportive of the
ORR and ROM data. Specifically, the data quality was sufficient at Week 25, and there
was an early, sustained, and statistically significant (at Week 25) difference observed
between vimseltinib and placebo. Interpretation of these results is limited by the
clinically meaningful change thresholds as described in detail below regarding PROMISPF.

Persistence of Effect
The Applicant’s Position:
No long-term efficacy data, with the exception of those presented in the preceding
sections, are available at the time of this application.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. Refer to FDA’s assessment of Efficacy
Results – Primary Endpoint (Section 8.1.2) for additional details. During the Type B preNDA meeting on January 16, 2024, FDA requested an additional 6 months of follow-up
duration data from the Applicant which was submitted with the original NDA
submission. The additional 6 months of follow-up data had a higher percentage of
patients with a 6 months (85%) and 9 months (58%) duration of response.
Data:
The ‘Efficacy Results – Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints’ sections above
provide details of the COA endpoints of ROM, physical function, worst stiffness, and
BPI-30.
The minimal clinically important difference (MCID) threshold for active ROM,
PROMIS-PF, and Worst Stiffness NRS were determined based on a subset of the
participants reaching end of Part 1 using blinded data. The MCID value for EQ-VAS was
based on a change of 7 points based on published literature (Pickard et al, 2007).
Distribution- and anchor-based methods were used to calculate potential thresholds based
on feedback from the FDA. Distribution-based methods included examining one-half and
one-quarter of the baseline standard deviation in the corresponding measure. Anchorbased methods examined the corresponding measure based on how
participants/Investigators responded to the “anchor” questionnaire. Anchor
questionnaires included patient global impression of severity (PGIS-PF), patient global
impression of change (PGIC-PF), PGIC overall, clinical global impression of severityROM, clinical global impression of change-ROM, PGIS-ROM, and PGIC-ROM. The
final proposed threshold was triangulated using the potential distribution and anchorbased thresholds and informed by the results of the qualitative interviews of the
participants conducted within 28 days before the End of Part 1 study visit. These
qualitative interviews were part of a cross-sectional substudy embedded into the
MOTION study. The responder definitions that were determined using this method is
robust and contributes to interpretation of clinically meaningful difference in patientreported outcome (PRO) scores between the treatment arms.
Missing data for any of the COAs was minimal, and there were no meaningful
differences in the rates of missing data between treatment arms. In a separate sensitivity
analysis, if the missing data for all participants were imputed based on the mean value
158
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
using multiple imputation for participants that received placebo, then the differences in
LS mean change from baseline at Week 25 remained significant for all PRO measures
(Table 32. The results demonstrate robustness to the conclusion that participants
receiving vimseltinib had statistically significant greater improvement in active ROM,
PROMIS-PF, Worst Stiffness NRS, and EQ-5D-5L VAS compared to those receiving
placebo, regardless of the true underlying missing data mechanism.
The Applicant’s Position:
ROM, physical function, stiffness, and pain are concepts that are important and
meaningful to patients with TGCT and the clinician-reported outcome and PRO measures
in MOTION are considered fit-for-purpose assessments of these concepts. Vimseltinib
treatment resulted in significant and meaningful improvements based on predetermined
MCIDs in function as measured by active ROM and PROMIS-PF; in symptomatic
improvements as measured by Worst Stiffness NRS and Worst Pain NRS; and in QoL as
measured by EQ-VAS.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The meaningful change thresholds for active ROM assessment, 15-item PROMIS-PF,
and Worst Stiffness NRS were determined based on a subset of the participants reaching
the end of Part 1 (Week 25) using blinded data. Distribution- and anchor-based methods
triangulated with patient exit interviews were used to calculate potential thresholds.
FDA recommends the use of anchor-based methods to establish meaningful withinpatient change in COA-based endpoint scores, although other methods (e.g., qualitative
evidence) can be used. Distribution-based methods are considered supportive to anchorbased methods.
Based on the submitted qualitative data, FDA agrees that the active ROM assessment (as
measured by a goniometer) and the 15-item PROMIS-PF assesses relevant and important
aspects of TGCT (i.e., active ROM and physical function, respectively).
FDA was unable to fully evaluate the reliability of the active ROM assessment due to
insufficient evidence provided by the Applicant. While the Applicant used multiple
anchors to conduct anchor-based analyses, the anchors have several limitations that
impact the interpretability of the results of the anchor-based analyses. Despite the
limitations of the anchors, the FDA evaluated the distributions of the primary anchor (i.e.,
PGIS-ROM item score) at baseline and Week 25. However, the sample size is small with
some missing data (24.4%) at Week 25 for PGIS-ROM. Therefore, the distributions of
the PGIS-ROM score at baseline and Week 25 needs to be interpreted with some caution.
Based on this data, as well as the limitations in the PGIS-ROM and PGIC-ROM items, it
is difficult to interpret the anchor-based analyses results. As such, it is unknown what
159
Version date: March 1, 2024 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5532076

4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
constitutes a meaningful change in the ROM assessment and FDA cannot agree with the
Applicant’s proposed meaningful change threshold in the active ROM assessment.
However, the magnitude of difference between arms, statistical significance, and results
from other endpoints including ORR and PROs provide supportive evidence that these
ROM results may be clinically relevant and meaningful.
Altered range of motion can significantly impact a person’s quality of life. In patients
with TGCT, this can often lead to pain, stiffness, swelling, and limited range of motion
leading to a diminished quality of life. Many times, previous treatment, including
surgery, can worsen ROM over time, especially for those patients with dTGCT. The
results for ROM demonstrate a clinically meaningful impact on patients who received
vimseltinib versus placebo despite the limitations described above and inclusion of ROM
results in product labeling was appropriate. The ROM results translate into an
improvement in active ROM and support the ORR results. Overall, patients treated with
vimseltinib had improved ROM, with a higher overall degree of improved ROM
demonstrated in the patients treated with vimseltinib compared to placebo.
FDA cannot make a definitive conclusion whether the 15-item PROMIS PF measure is
well-understood and interpreted appropriately in this context due to the limited approach
used by the Applicant to evaluate participant comprehension.
The Applicant did not propose a target anchor change category based on the PGISPhysical Function item. The Applicant provided insufficient evidence to support the
target anchor change category on the PGIC-Physical Function based on the approach
used in their exit interview study. Refer to the full COA review. To take a more
conservative approach, FDA recommends using a 2-category change on the PGISPhysical Function item and the “Much improved” response category on the PGICPhysical Function item as the target anchor change category. Based on this, patients who
experienced a 2-category change in the PGIS-Physical Function item had a mean change
in the 15-item PROMIS Physical Function score of 7.3 and a median change of 6.5.
Patients who reported “Much improved” in the PGIC-Physical Function item had a mean
change in the 15-item PROMIS Physical Function score of 4.9 and a median change of
5.0.
The Applicant additionally inspected the eCDF curves for change from baseline to Week
25 in 15-item PROMIS Physical Function score by change in the PGIS-Physical Function
and PGIC-Physical Function items. However, the graphs were difficult to interpret due to
the small sample size at Week 25 (e.g., poor distribution across response categories).
FDA was unable to determine with confidence a threshold of clinically meaningful
change in the 15-item PROMIS Physical Function score based on the submitted anchorbased analyses. However, as seen in Figure 21 change from baseline in the 15-item

DCC-3014-01-001 (Phase 1/2 Supportive Study)
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
DCC-3014-01-001 (Phase 1/2 study) is an ongoing open-label Phase 1/2 study of
vimseltinib in participants with advanced tumors. The cutoff for the efficacy data
presented in this review is 27 Jun 2023.
The study comprised 2 distinct parts: dose escalation (which enrolled both participants
with MST and participants with TGCT [Phase 1]) and expansion (which enrolled
participants with TGCT only [Phase 2]).
The Dose Escalation Phase design is provided in Figure 22Figure 22. Dose escalation
was based on a pharmacologically guided 3+3 study design in participants with MST and
TGCT. The maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was defined as the highest dose level at
which no more than 1 of 6 dose limiting toxicity (DLT)-evaluable participants (<33%)
experienced a DLT(s) in Cycle 1 during dose escalation. The recommended phase 2 dose
(RP2D) may be the MTD or a biologically active or maximally feasible dose that is lower
than the MTD.

Schematic Illustration of Dose Escalation, Phase 1/2 Study

50% from that set in the previous cohort. An additional lower or intermediate dose level(s) and/or alternate dose schedule(s)
could be explored to determine an RP2D in participants with TGCT. More than one cohort at lower dose levels could be run
simultaneously. Additional participants could be enrolled to a Dose Escalation Cohort for further evaluation of safety, efficacy,
PK, and pharmacodynamics.

Upon determination of the RP2D, 2 expansion cohorts of participants with TGCT were
initiated for further evaluation of safety, tolerability, efficacy, PK, pharmacodynamics,
and efficacy to support any future study of vimseltinib in this population (Cohort A and
Cohort B).
All participants in the Dose Expansion Phase received vimseltinib at the RP2D (30 mg
twice weekly on Day 1 and Day 5 with no loading dose at the same time each week). A

data monitoring committee monitored the safety and efficacy data in both phases of the
study on a periodic basis to ensure the ongoing safety of study participants.
The study consisted of a screening period conducted within 28 days (Dose Escalation
Phase) or 42 days (Expansion Phase) prior to the first dose of study drug, a treatment
period of 28-day cycles, an end of treatment visit, and a Follow-up Safety visit 30 days
(±5 days) after the last dose of study drug. Participants were then followed in the Disease
Follow-up period for up to 2 years after last dose of study treatment or until radiological
progression, start of new subsequent therapy/surgery, or withdrawal of consent.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on the information presented by the Applicant, the reviewer agrees with
Applicant’s description of the supportive study. The maximum administered dose of
vimseltinib in patients with TGCT was 30 mg twice weekly following a 30 mg x 5 day
loading dose. All patients in the Dose Expansion Part received vimseltinib 30 mg twice
weekly on Day 1 and Day 5 with no loading dose. A total of 66 patients with TGCT
received the RP2D of 30 mg twice weekly. An additional 32 patients with TGCT were
treated at other doses.

The reviewer notes that the a few key eligibility criteria for DCC-3014-01-001 differ
from MOTION. In the original DCC-3014-01-001 study, patients were required to have
bilirubin 1.5 times the upper limit of normal (ULN); AST/ALT 3×ULN to be eligible
to enroll. Throughout the course of the study, amendments to the protocol were made to
reflect the possibility of hepatotoxicity. With protocol amendment 5, patients with TGCT
were eligible if bilirubin 1.5 times the ULN; and AST/ALT 1.5×ULN. In protocol
amendment 6, patients with TGCT had to have bilirubin, AST/ALT ULN and patients
with active liver or biliary disease including evidence of fatty liver, nonalcoholic
steatohepatitis (NASH), or cirrhosis were excluded. Within the original MOTION
protocol, these stricter parameters for eligibility were already in place.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with Applicant’s descriptions of endpoints. TGCT is a slow-growing
progressive and debilitating but non-lethal disease. ORR can be used to measure the
reduction in tumor burden over time. Time to event endpoints such as progression0free
survival and overall survival and PRO endpoints are not interpretable in a single-arm
study.

c. New schedule of assessment was added for patients with solid tumors and
patients with TGCT.

for evaluation of anti-tumor activity in patients with dTGCT.

added.

Patient Disposition
Data:
A total of 134 participants were enrolled in the study prior to the data cutoff of
27 Jun 2023. The Dose Escalation Phase enrolled 69 participants overall (37 participants
with MST, 32 with TGCT), and the Dose Expansion Phase enrolled 65 participants with
TGCT (46 participants in Cohort A, 19 in Cohort B).
The Applicant’s Position:
At the data cutoff, a total of 51 (52.6%) participants with TGCT were ongoing study
treatment and 57 (58.8%) remained in the study. The most common reason for
discontinuation of treatment was withdrawal by participant (26 [26.8%]). The most
common reason for study discontinuation was also withdrawal by participant
(16 [16.5%]). At the time of data cutoff, 2 participants with TGCT in each phase were
considered to have completed the study.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently review the patient disposition data. A total of 97 patients with
TGCT enrolled in the study and 65 received the RP2D.
Protocol Violations/Deviations
Data:
Protocol deviations were recorded for 41 (42.3%) participants with TGCT overall.
The Applicant’s Position:
Of the 41 participants, 2 reported in participants with TGCT in the Dose Expansion Phase
were considered IPDs.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify this results section and cannot comment on the
potential for these protocol deviations to influence the results of the supportive study.

Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
Data:
Frequently reported medical history preferred terms (PTs) for participants with TGCT
included arthralgia (54.6%), hypertension (23.7%), menopause (13.4%), joint stiffness
(12.4%), and joint swelling (12.4%).
The majority of participants with TGCT had diffuse-type TGCT, with the most common
location in the knee or ankle. The mean (STD) overall time from the original TGCT
diagnosis to the first dose of vimseltinib was 5.88 (6.817) years. No participant had
known metastatic disease (99.0% no metastatic disease, 1.0% unknown).
The Applicant’s Position:
The medical and TGCT history is representative of the intended patient population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that the medical and TGCT history of patients in DCC-3014-01-001 is
generally consistent with the proposed patient population.

Data:

The median total treatment duration in participants with TGCT was 537.0 (range: 6 to
1427) days; median number of cycles was 20.0 (range: 1 to 51). Median RDI was 75.00%
(range: 25.0% to 106.3%) for participants with TGCT.
Concomitant Medications
The majority of participants with TGCT reported at least one prior or concomitant
medication and/or non-drug therapy. Frequently reported prior medications included
paracetamol (18.6% of participants) and ibuprofen (14.4%). Common concomitant

Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint (Including Sensitivity Analyses)
Data:
Objective Response at Week 25 (C7D1) by IRR Using RECIST v1.1 (Expansion Cohort
A)
In Cohort A (Expansion Phase, 30 mg twice weekly), ORR at Week 25 (C7D1) as
assessed by IRR using RECIST v1.1 was 38% (95% CI: 24%, 53%); 0 participants had
achieved CR, and 17 had achieved PR. Results of a sensitivity assessment of investigator
review using RECIST v1.1 were consistent with the primary analysis.
Duration of Response by IRR Using RECIST v1.1 (Expansion Cohort A)
During the entire treatment period to data cutoff, ORR in participants with TGCT in
Cohort A was 64% (95% CI: 49%, 78%) by IRR using RECIST v1.1. Median DOR was
not reached because all responses were ongoing at the time of analysis.
The Applicant’s Position:
Vimseltinib demonstrated efficacy in participants with TGCT with no prior specific anti
CSF1/CSF1R therapy (except imatinib or nilotinib) at the RP2D.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA did not independently verify the efficacy results of this study, as the evaluation of
efficacy in the proposed indication for vimseltinib is based on MOTION. In DCC-301401-001, 97 patients with TGCT were treated with vimseltinib, including 65 patients
treated at a dose of 30 mg twice weekly. The results presented by the Applicant

Data Quality and Integrity
Data:
All participant data relating to the study was entered into the electronic CRFs unless
transmitted to the sponsor or designee electronically (e.g., laboratory data). The
investigator was responsible for verifying that data entries were accurate and correct by
electronically signing the CRF. The investigator maintained accurate documentation
(source data) that supported the information entered in the CRF. The investigator
permitted studyဨrelated monitoring, audits, Institutional Review Board (IRB) /
Independent Ethics Committee (IEC) review, and regulatory agency inspections and
provided direct access to source data documents. Monitoring details describing strategy
methods, responsibilities, and requirements, including handling of noncompliance issues
and monitoring techniques (onဨsite, remote monitoring) were indicated in the monitoring
plan to ensure the protocol and GCP was followed.
The sponsor or designee was responsible for the data management of this study including
quality checking of the data. The sponsor assumed accountability for actions delegated to
other individuals (e.g., Contract Research Organizations).
Study monitors performed ongoing source data verification to confirm that data entered
into the eCRF by authorized site personnel were accurate, complete, and verifiable from
source documents; that the safety and rights of participants were being protected; and that
the study was being conducted in accordance with the currently approved protocol and
any other study agreements, ICH GCP, and all applicable regulatory requirements.
Records and documents, including signed ICFs, pertaining to the conduct of this study
were retained by the investigator according to specifications in the ICH guidelines, local
regulations, or as specified in the clinical trial agreement, whichever was longer. No
records were to be destroyed during the retention period without the written approval of
the sponsor. No records were to be transferred to another location or party without
written notification to the sponsor. Deciphera conducts clinical studies according to
procedures that incorporate the ethical principles of GCP. To ensure compliance with
these procedures and to assess the adequacy of quality control procedures, Deciphera
undertakes a GCP audit program. Audits were performed by Deciphera Quality
Assurance (or designees) operating independently of the study monitors. The audits were
aimed at study documentation, investigator sites, and CSRs. The audit program, together
with Deciphera’s internal quality control procedures, provide reassurance that study
conclusions were based on valid procedures for data management and analysis, and that
the clinical study program was carried out in accordance with GCP guidelines.

Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints
Data:
Objective Tumor Response at Week 25 (C7D1) by IRR Using TVS (Expansion Cohort A)
In Cohort A, ORR at Week 25 (C7D1) assessed by IRR using TVS was 51% (95% CI:
36%, 66%); 0 participants had achieved CR, and 23 had achieved PR. During the overall
treatment period, ORR assessed by IRR using TVS was 64% (95% CI: 49%, 78%) in
Cohort A; 0 participants experienced CR, and 29 experienced PR. Median DOR could
not be estimated by Kaplan-Meier for TVS as no participants had progressed or died
using TVS at the data cutoff.
Functional Assessment (Expansion Cohort A)
Active relative ROM across a variety of joints increased by 19.74 (STD: 43.483)
percentage points from Baseline to Week 25 (C7D1) in participants in Cohort A.
Patient-reported Outcomes (Expansion Cohort A)
BPI Worst Pain Response (Expansion Cohort A)
In Cohort A, at Week 25 (C7D1) 50.0% (95% CI: 34.9%, 65.1%) of participants were
considered BPI worst pain responders (i.e., experienced a decrease of at least 30% in the
mean BPI worst pain item and did not experience a 30% or greater increase in narcotic
analgesic use).
PROMIS-PF Questionnaire (Expansion Cohort A)
Mean (STD) improvement from Baseline in PROMIS-PF for all participants with TGCT
in Cohort A was 3.9 (7.83) points at Week 25 (C7D1).
Worst Stiffness NRS (Expansion Cohort A)
Participants with TGCT in Cohort A showed a mean (STD) improvement in worst
stiffness in the last 24 hours by NRS of 1.81 (2.676) points by Week 25 (C7D1).

from MOTION and the corresponding endpoints (as applicable) from the Phase 1/2 study

participants with other solid tumors. Therefore, only efficacy results from participants

(36%).

to the MOTION vimseltinib arm (40%) and in DCC-3014-01-001 Dose Expansion

Cohort A includes patients with no prior treatment with a CSF1 or CSF1R targeted agent.
FDA did not independently verify the efficacy results of DCC-3014-01-001, as the
efficacy of vimseltinib in patients with TGCT is based on MOTION.

Objective Response Rate per Tumor Volume Score at Week 25

had CR and 52 (63%) had PR.

blinded IRR in Phase 1/2 Dose Escalation Cohort 5 was 38% and in Dose Expansion
Cohort B was 29%.

DOR per RECIST 1.1 and TVS for the MOTION double-blind period, primary analysis
with a cutoff of 22 Aug 2023, are presented in Section 8.1.2. Tumor responses were long
